01. Loop Format and the Senior Bar: Airbnb Senior MLE (Experimentation / A/B Testing)

Research note (read first). This chapter went through an adversarial review on 2026-10-14. During that review the egress proxy blocked igotanoffer.com, glassdoor.com, leetcode.com, medium.com, arxiv.org, dl.acm.org and careers.airbnb.com, and the shared web-search budget was already used up. Only one source could be fetched and checked line by line: the community-collected Airbnb question list on GitHub [11]. Every question tagged [Reported] below is supported by that verified source. Questions tagged [Reported, unverified] are attributed to Airbnb by sources [1]-[5], which could not be re-fetched in this review. Treat them as plausible but unconfirmed. [Likely] means an inference from the role, the level and Airbnb's products, not a candidate report. Source [11] was compiled around 2017-2020 and says Airbnb may have rotated its question pool since, so the 2024-2026 loop could not be confirmed here. A recruiter's prep packet always overrides this chapter.

Format notes

Stage Typical length Interviewer What is assessed (Senior / L5 bar)
Recruiter call 20-30 min Recruiter Motivation ("Why Airbnb?"), level calibration (L5 = Senior), team fit (Search/Ranking, Pricing, Trust, Payments, Relevance, Experimentation platform), comp, timeline [1][2]
Technical phone screen 45-60 min Engineer One or two practical coding problems on a shared editor (CoderPad-style). The code is expected to compile and run against test cases. Pseudocode does not pass [1][2][3]
Onsite: Coding x2 45-60 min each Engineers Runnable, clean, tested code. One round is often algorithmic (LeetCode-medium/hard, many Airbnb-tagged problems). The other is a longer "practical" problem with evolving requirements (parsing, pagination, scheduling, simulation) [1][3][4][11]
Onsite: ML design 45-60 min Senior/Staff MLE End-to-end ML system for an Airbnb product (search ranking, pricing, fraud, recommendations), including offline/online evaluation and experiment design [Likely; 6-10]
Onsite: System design 45-60 min Senior/Staff engineer Scalable services and data pipelines (for example a booking system, an experimentation/assignment service, a feature store) [1][2]
Onsite: Past project deep dive 45-60 min Hiring manager or senior IC Ownership, technical depth, trade-offs and measured impact of a project you led. Sometimes presented with slides [1][2]
Onsite: Core values x2 30-45 min each Employees outside the hiring team (often non-engineers) Airbnb's four core values: Champion the Mission, Be a Host, Embrace the Adventure, Be a "Cereal" Entrepreneur. A strong fail here can veto an otherwise strong loop [1][2][5]
Debrief / hiring committee n/a Panel + leadership review Calibrated on level. Senior candidates are expected to drive ambiguity, lead cross-functional work and show measured impact [1][2]

Key expectations that candidates consistently report:

  • Runnable code is mandatory. Interviewers run your code. Syntax errors, unhandled edge cases or no tests count against you even if the logic is right [1][3].
  • Practical over puzzle. Problems are often framed as real product scenarios: listings pagination, host search results, price rounding, travel itineraries, calendar availability [3][4][11].
  • Core values are scored as seriously as the technical rounds and are run by people with no stake in the team [1][5].
  • Senior bar (L5): you independently own an ambiguous problem end to end. You make clear trade-offs (latency vs. quality, bias vs. variance in experiment estimates). You influence partners such as Product, Data Science and Design, and you mentor others. Coding and design are expected to be done without heavy hints [Likely; 1][2].
  • Experimentation-flavored MLE roles: Airbnb runs a mature in-house experimentation platform (ERF) and has written publicly about A/B testing pitfalls, interleaving for search ranking and two-sided-marketplace effects. Expect experimentation probes inside the ML design and past-project rounds [6][7][8][9].

Questions

A. Recruiter call and process logistics

  1. Why Airbnb, and why now? [Reported, unverified] [1][2]
    • Which Airbnb product surface (Search, Pricing, Trust, Experiences, Payments) would you most want to work on, and why?
    • Have you used Airbnb as a guest or as a host? What would you change?
  2. Walk me through your background in under three minutes, focusing on ML in production. [Reported, unverified] [1]
  3. What level are you targeting, and what scope of work did you own at your current level? [Likely]
    • How many engineers or scientists did you lead or influence on your largest project?
  4. Which programming language will you use in coding rounds, and are you fluent enough to run code without IDE help? [Likely] [3]
  5. How much of your recent work was modeling, and how much was infrastructure or experimentation? [Likely]
  6. Have you designed or analyzed online A/B tests yourself, or did a data scientist own that? [Likely]
  7. What are your compensation expectations and timeline, and do you have competing processes? [Reported, unverified] [2]
  8. Are you open to team matching across Search/Relevance, Pricing, Trust and the Experimentation platform? [Likely]

B. Technical phone screen (practical coding, must run)

  1. Display page: given CSV strings of search results (host_id, listing_id, score, city) sorted by score and a page size of 12, reorder them so a host appears at most once per page if possible, otherwise preserving order, and print the pages separated by a blank line. [Reported] [11]
    • What happens on the last page when only one host's listings remain?
    • What is the time complexity, and can you do it in a single pass?
  2. Round prices: round each price to its floor or ceiling so the rounded sum equals Round(sum of prices), minimizing the total absolute rounding error. [Reported] [11]
    • What if the target is unreachable? How do you signal that?
    • How would you handle currency with two decimal places?
  3. Pour water: given terrain heights, a water quantity and a pour position, simulate the droplets and print the final terrain with water. [Reported] [11]
    • How do you test the boundary indices?
  4. Text justification: fully justify lines of words to a fixed width. [Reported] [11]
    • How do you handle a single word on a line, and the last line?
  5. List-of-lists iterator with hasNext, next and remove (remove the last returned element; calling it twice is an error). [Reported] [11]
  6. Implement a CSV parser that handles quoted fields, escaped quotes and commas inside quotes. [Reported] [11]
    • How do you handle multi-line quoted fields?
  7. Given an IPv4 address and a count, return the minimal list of CIDR blocks covering exactly that range. [Reported] [11]
  8. Write tests for your solution right now. Which cases did you choose, and why? [Likely] [3]
  9. Collatz conjecture: for every start value in [1, n], find the maximum number of steps to reach 1. [Reported] [11]
    • How would memoization change the runtime, and how do you bound memory for large n?
  10. Implement a queue backed by a set of fixed-size arrays (no dynamic array resizing). [Reported] [11]
  11. Travel buddy: a friend whose city wishlist overlaps yours by more than 50% (of their list) is a buddy. Return buddies sorted by similarity. [Reported] [11]
    • Extend it to recommend up to N cities from buddies' wishlists.
  12. Find case combinations of a string: generate all upper/lower case variants and test each against a hidden-string checker API. [Reported] [11]
    • How do you limit API calls when the checker is expensive?
  13. Finding ocean: given a map of L (land) and W (water) and a starting ocean cell, mark all connected ocean cells as O and print the map. [Reported] [11]
    • When does recursive DFS fail on large maps, and what do you replace it with?
  14. Guess the number: a secret 4-digit code with digits 1-6 sits behind an API that returns the count of correctly placed digits. Find the code with as few calls as possible. [Reported] [11]

C. Onsite coding round 1 (algorithmic, Airbnb-tagged style)

  1. Alien dictionary: derive a character ordering from a sorted list of words. [Reported, unverified] [4]
    • How do you detect an invalid ordering, such as a prefix appearing after a longer word?
  2. Palindrome pairs: find all index pairs whose concatenation forms a palindrome. [Reported] [11]
    • Can you do better than O(n^2 * k)?
  3. Minimum cost with at most K stops: given flights (from, to, price), find the cheapest itinerary from source to destination. [Reported] [11]
    • What changes if edges carry both price and duration and you must optimize a weighted objective?
  4. Sliding game: find a sequence of moves that solves a 3x3 eight-tile puzzle. [Reported] [11]
    • How does your search change for an unsolvable board, and how do you detect it up front?
  5. String pyramid transition: given the bottom row and a map of allowed parents for each (left, right) pair, can the pyramid reach a root? [Reported] [11]
  6. Meeting time: given everyone's meeting intervals, return the common free intervals. [Reported] [11]
    • How would you adapt this to host calendars with blocked dates and minimum-night rules?
  7. Smallest common region: find the lowest common region in a region hierarchy. [Likely] [4]
  8. Menu combination sum: find all combinations of menu item prices that sum to a budget K. [Reported] [11]
    • How do you handle float prices without precision bugs?
  9. Simple regex matching supporting ., * and +. [Reported] [11]
  10. Boggle game: on a letter grid with a dictionary, find the path that contains the largest number of dictionary words. [Reported] [11]
  11. Basic calculator with + - * / and parentheses. [Likely] [4]
  12. Contains duplicate within distance k and within value difference t. [Likely] [4]
  13. Mini parser for nested lists (nested-list iterator). [Likely] [4]
  14. Maximum nights you can accommodate: given consecutive-night booking requests, pick bookings that maximize occupied nights, leaving at least one day gap between bookings for cleaning. [Reported] [11]
    • What if bookings are intervals with arbitrary overlaps (weighted interval scheduling)?
  15. K edit distance: find all dictionary words within edit distance k of a target word. [Reported] [11]
    • How would a trie cut the work compared with checking each word?
  16. Preference list: merge everyone's ordered city preferences into one list consistent with all of them. [Reported] [11]
    • What do you return when the preferences contain a cycle?
  17. Ten wizards: each wizard knows a list of others, and a hop from i to j costs (i - j)^2. Find the minimum-cost path from wizard 0 to wizard 9. [Reported] [11]
  18. Minimum vertices to traverse a directed graph: return the smallest set of start vertices from which every vertex is reachable. [Reported] [11]
  19. Find the median of a file of integers too large for memory, with only sequential access. [Reported] [11]
  20. Hilbert curve: given (x, y) and an iteration count, return the step index of that point along the curve. [Reported] [11]
  21. Number of intersecting rectangles: given many axis-aligned rectangles, count how many groups of them intersect. [Reported] [11]

D. Onsite coding round 2 (practical, evolving requirements)

  1. Design and implement a booking calendar that supports book(start, end), rejects overlaps and answers is_available(start, end). [Likely] [3]
    • Now add per-night pricing and return the total price for a stay.
    • Now support split stays across two listings when no single listing is available.
  2. Implement a simple key-value store with nested transactions (begin, commit, rollback). [Likely] [3]
  3. Parse a log of experiment exposures (user_id, experiment, variant, timestamp) and output per-variant unique user counts, de-duplicating users exposed to multiple variants. [Likely]
    • How would you flag and count users with conflicting assignments?
  4. Implement deterministic hash-based bucketing: given user_id, experiment salt and traffic split, return a variant. [Likely] [6]
    • How do you guarantee independence between experiments run on the same users?
    • How would you ramp traffic from 1% to 50% without reshuffling already-exposed users?
  5. Given streaming events, compute a rolling 7-day conversion rate per listing with bounded memory. [Likely]
  6. Implement a file system with create(path, value) and get(path), returning an error when a parent path does not exist. [Reported] [11]
    • Add a watch(path, callback) that fires on changes to the path or its children.
  7. Given guest reviews with ratings and timestamps, return the top-K listings by a time-decayed average rating. [Likely]
    • How do you handle listings with very few reviews (shrinkage)?
  8. Implement an LRU or LFU cache for search results, then extend it with a TTL. [Likely]
  9. Refactor your solution so that a new requirement (for example multi-currency support) fits in with minimal change. How do you structure the code? [Likely] [3]
  10. Simulate Diplomacy: given orders per army (move, hold, support), resolve the board, where stronger armies win a contested location and ties eliminate both. [Reported] [11]
    • A new rule arrives mid-interview: a support is cut if the supporting army is attacked. How do you add it without rewriting the resolver?
  11. Given per-user rows (variant, converted, pre-period bookings), write runnable code that returns the lift and a 95% confidence interval for each treatment arm. [Likely] [6]
    • Add a CUPED adjustment using the pre-period covariate and show the variance reduction on the test data.
  12. Given daily assignment counts per variant, write a function that flags sample ratio mismatch against the intended split. [Likely] [6]

E. ML design round (Airbnb product + experimentation)

  1. Design the search ranking system for Airbnb stays. [Likely] [7][8][9]
    • What are your labels (bookings, clicks, long clicks, host acceptances), and how do you weight them?
    • How do you handle position bias in training data?
    • How do you evaluate offline (NDCG on booked listings) versus online?
  2. Design real-time personalization using listing embeddings from in-session clicks. [Likely] [8]
    • How would you inject booking or market signals into the embedding objective?
    • How do you handle cold-start listings with no sessions?
  3. Design a dynamic pricing suggestion model for hosts (Smart Pricing). [Likely]
    • How do you estimate demand elasticity when price is confounded with seasonality and quality?
    • How do you A/B test a pricing model when hosts can override it?
  4. Design a fraud or risk model for bookings (Trust and Safety). [Likely]
    • How do you evaluate a model whose positive labels arrive weeks late and are censored by your own interventions?
  5. Design "similar listings" or "recommended experiences" recommendations. [Likely]
  6. Design a model that predicts host acceptance probability and use it in ranking. [Likely] [7]
  7. Design an ML system to detect low-quality or misleading listing photos and text. [Likely]
  8. How would you design the online experiment that launches your new ranking model? [Likely] [6][9]
    • What is the primary metric, and what are your guardrail metrics?
    • What unit of randomization would you use (user, session, listing, market), and why?
    • How do you estimate the required sample size and duration?
  9. In a two-sided marketplace, why can a user-randomized test of a ranking change be biased? [Likely] [6]
    • How would listing-side or market-level (cluster) randomization change your estimates?
    • What is interference, or a SUTVA violation, in this setting?
  10. When would you use interleaving instead of A/B testing for search ranking? [Likely] [9]
    • How does interleaving sensitivity compare with A/B, and what can interleaving not measure?
  11. Offline NDCG improved but the online A/B showed no booking lift. How do you debug this? [Likely] [7]
  12. Bookings happen weeks after search. How do you choose an experiment metric that is sensitive enough but still aligned with long-term value? [Likely] [6]
    • How would you build and validate a proxy or surrogate metric?
  13. How would you apply variance reduction (CUPED or regression adjustment) to booking-conversion experiments? [Likely] [6]
  14. How do you handle novelty effects and seasonality (holidays, events) when reading test results? [Likely]
  15. How do you control false discoveries when a team runs dozens of concurrent experiments and checks results daily? [Likely] [6]
    • What is the peeking problem, and how do sequential tests or dynamic p-value thresholds address it?
  16. How would you measure the long-term effect of a model change after the A/B test has ended (holdouts)? [Likely]
  17. Your model improves guest conversion but reduces host earnings in some markets. How do you decide whether to ship? [Likely]
  18. The ranking team wants to ship a model every two weeks, but each booking-metric A/B test needs four weeks. How do you redesign the evaluation pipeline? [Likely] [6][9]
  19. Design an ML model that predicts which experiments are likely to produce a false positive (for example, from metric variance, traffic and peeking history) so the platform can warn owners. [Likely] [6]

F. System design round

  1. Design an experimentation assignment and logging service used by every Airbnb client (web, iOS, Android, backend). [Likely] [6]
    • How do you keep assignment consistent across logged-out and logged-in states?
    • How do you guarantee exposure logging is not lost or double-counted?
  2. Design the metrics computation pipeline for an experimentation platform (daily scorecards for thousands of metrics). [Likely] [6]
    • How do you backfill when a metric definition changes?
  3. Design a booking and reservation system that prevents double-booking under high concurrency. [Likely] [1]
  4. Design a feature store that serves both offline training and low-latency online ranking. [Likely]
    • How do you prevent training-serving skew?
  5. Design a search service that retrieves and ranks listings within 200 ms p99 across millions of listings. [Likely] [7]
    • Where do you place candidate retrieval, filtering (availability, price, map bounds) and ML re-ranking?
  6. Design a model serving and rollout system with shadow mode, canary and automatic rollback. [Likely]
  7. Design a notification system for hosts and guests that respects time zones and rate limits. [Likely]
  8. Design a calendar availability service for millions of listings with frequent updates and iCal sync. [Likely]
  9. Design a feature-flag and kill-switch service that lets an on-call engineer turn off a misbehaving model in every region within one minute. [Likely]

G. Past project deep dive

  1. Walk me through the ML project you are proudest of, from problem framing to measured impact. [Reported, unverified] [1][2]
    • What was your specific contribution compared with the team's?
    • What alternatives did you reject, and why?
  2. How did you measure the impact of that project? Was there an A/B test, and what did it show? [Likely]
    • What were the confidence intervals, and were there any surprising segment-level results?
  3. What was the hardest technical trade-off you made on that project? [Likely]
  4. If you had to redo the project with half the time, what would you cut? [Likely]
  5. Tell me about a model that worked offline but failed in production. What did you learn? [Likely]
  6. How did you get alignment from Product and Data Science on the success metric? [Likely]
  7. What would the next iteration of that system look like? [Likely]
  8. How did you handle data quality or labeling problems in that project? [Likely]
  9. Which part of the system would break first at 10x scale? [Likely]
  10. Your project's A/B test showed a lift that later disappeared in the holdout. Walk me through how you found the cause. [Likely]

H. Core values interviews (two rounds, cross-functional)

  1. Which of Airbnb's core values resonates with you most, and why? [Reported, unverified] [1][5]
  2. Tell me about a time you went above and beyond for a customer, user or teammate ("Be a Host"). [Reported, unverified] [1][5]
  3. Tell me about a time you took a big risk or tried something no one had done before ("Embrace the Adventure"). [Reported, unverified] [1][5]
  4. Tell me about a time you had to be scrappy and resourceful with limited resources ("Be a Cereal Entrepreneur"). [Reported, unverified] [1][5]
  5. What does "belonging" mean to you, and when have you created it for someone else ("Champion the Mission")? [Reported, unverified] [1][5]
  6. Describe a memorable travel or hosting experience and what it taught you. [Reported, unverified] [1]
  7. Tell me about a time you failed. What did you do next? [Reported, unverified] [1][2]
  8. Tell me about a time you disagreed with a teammate or manager. How did you resolve it? [Reported, unverified] [2]
  9. Tell me about a time you received difficult feedback. [Reported, unverified] [2]
  10. Describe a time you helped someone grow or mentored a junior colleague. [Likely]
  11. Tell me about a time you made a decision that was right for users but unpopular internally. [Likely]
  12. How do you build inclusive teams or include people with different perspectives? [Likely] [5]
  13. Tell me about a time you had to adapt to a big change you did not choose (a reorg, layoffs, a pivot). [Likely]

I. Senior-level calibration probes (appear across rounds)

  1. How do you decide when a simpler heuristic should ship instead of an ML model? [Likely]
  2. Tell me about a time you set the technical direction for a team without formal authority. [Likely]
  3. How do you prioritize between model quality, latency and engineering cost? [Likely]
  4. Describe a time you stopped or reversed a launch because the experiment results were ambiguous. [Likely]
  5. How do you communicate a negative or inconclusive A/B result to leadership? [Likely]
  6. How do you make sure your experiment results are reproducible and trusted by other teams? [Likely]
  7. What metrics would you put on a dashboard to know your production model is healthy? [Likely]
  8. How do you balance short-term booking metrics against long-term guest and host trust? [Likely]
  9. A product manager wants to ship a feature whose test was stopped early at p = 0.04 after daily peeking. What do you tell them, and what do you change in the process? [Likely] [6]
  10. What questions do you have for us about the team, the experimentation culture or the roadmap? [Reported, unverified] [1]

02. Coding Rounds: Airbnb-Tagged LeetCode and Reported Practical Coding Questions

Research note (read first). First researched 2026-10-13; adversarially reviewed 2026-10-14. On review, web search was unavailable (the shared search budget was exhausted), and direct HTTP to GitHub returned proxy 403s, but pages could still be fetched through a fetch tool. About 50 cited pages were refetched and read on review; a few returned transient 503s and were left as cited. Glassdoor, Blind, LeetCode, Reddit, 1point3acres, Prepfully and Airbnb's own sites were not reachable. All evidence below comes from GitHub-hosted pages:

  • two open mirrors of LeetCode's Airbnb company tag [5][6];
  • two mirrored TrueInterview candidate-report guides, one for the MLE track and one for the SWE track [1][2];
  • a dated question tracker (Sep 2025 to Sep 2026) and a 2025-2026 company guide [3][4];
  • classic Airbnb question collections [7][8][9][10];
  • a GitHub mirror of about 100 1point3acres (一亩三分地) Airbnb interview posts [13]. Posts that carry a specific question are cited individually [14]-[58] and [59]-[82].

All of this is third-party, user-submitted material, so treat every [Reported] tag as "a candidate or aggregator says so", not as Airbnb confirmation. Several mirror files carry no role, level or date, and some do not name the company; they are attributed to Airbnb only because the mirror files them under an Airbnb folder. Where that is the only link to Airbnb, the question says so. [Likely] marks an inference from the MLE/experimentation role. It does not mean anyone reported it.

Format notes

  • How many coding rounds an MLE gets. The one public MLE-track summary (3 candidate reports, mainly 2026 Q2) gives MLEs one technical phone screen and typically one onsite coding round (45-60 min). It says the track is "filtered by design depth, not by code": the decisive rounds are 1-2 ML system design rounds [1]. One 1point3acres MLE-type loop had one coding round (LC 1298) plus two ML design rounds (LTV and host pricing) [14]. Another had two ML design rounds plus a "ConnectN" OOD coding round [15]. SWE-track loops usually have 1-2 coding rounds plus a separate code review round (about 3 PRs) [2][4]. One aggregator says code review replaced the second coding round around 2024, while some 2026 India loops still had two 45-minute coding rounds [4]. In several 2025-2026 loops, a weak code review round led to an added coding round or to rejection [59][72][77]. Ask your recruiter whether the MLE loop includes code review.
  • Runnable code with tests is the bar. Candidates report that Airbnb requires "bug free" code on the spot with all cases passing [20]. Other reports: code must run and be tested within 45 minutes [2]; there were "about 10 test cases" for Connect 4 [32]; problems arrived with stdin-style test strings [17]; one interviewer gave no interface, so the candidate had to agree the API and then write tests that pass [36]. Airbnb has long stressed "compilable, runnable code" [11]. One MLE screen expected the candidate to explain pruning or memoization before coding, then write test cases [1].
  • Shape of a round. A round is one practical problem in 45 min, often in two parts. Interviewers add constraints once part 1 runs, so leave 10-15 minutes for follow-ups [4][33]. One candidate instead got three short LeetCode problems in 60 minutes [47]. Several posts say the interviewer said nothing and just pasted the problem [18][20]. Communication still counts: a promotional prep guide claims that silent candidates score lower than ones who think aloud [12]. Switching algorithms mid-round and polishing helpers too early are cited as reasons for rejection [2].
  • Flavor. Problems are framed as product stories: split stays, group lodging, menus and combos, refunds, layovers, ski runs, host pagination, review highlighting. They test DP (often bitmask), graph search, simulation, parsing and small-object design rather than exotic tricks [1][3][4]. LeetCode's Airbnb tag over the last 6 months is led by Design Excel Sum Formula, Maximum Profit in Job Scheduling, Shortest Uncommon Substring, Maximum Candies from Boxes, Shopping Offers, Accounts Merge, Text Justification and Smallest Sufficient Team [5].
  • Senior bar. Seniors are expected to clarify ambiguous specs themselves, for example tie-breaking rules, whether the graph has cycles, and whether to return the path or only the cost [16]. They must also reach working code fast enough to discuss complexity and extensions. One candidate passed with a suboptimal solution that ran and passed every test, and only described the optimization [12][19]. Another failed because they could not beat O(N^2) when asked [19]. Several senior rejections were attributed to design or deep-dive rounds even when coding was strong [16][28].

Questions

A. Airbnb "classic" practical problems (long-running question bank)

  1. Display Page / pagination: given CSV search results (host_id, listing_id, score, city) sorted by score and a page size, reorder them so that a host appears at most once per page when possible, while otherwise preserving order. Print the pages as blocks. [Reported] [7][8][52]
    • What should happen when the remaining results all belong to one host?
    • Can you do it in one pass without repeatedly rescanning the list?
    • How would you test that the relative order inside a page is preserved?
  2. Text Justification: pack words greedily into lines of exactly width L, spreading extra spaces left-first, with the last line left-justified. [Reported] [5][7][8]
    • How do you handle a line that holds a single word?
    • What changes if words can be longer than L?
  3. Fixed-width text formatting (simpler variant: left-align and pad with trailing spaces), then lay the lines out into k side-by-side columns separated by one space. [Reported] [1][40][50]
    • When one column runs out of lines, what do you print in its place?
    • What is the complexity for n up to 1e5 words?
  4. Menu combination sum: given menu item prices, return every combination of items whose total is exactly the budget. Items may repeat, and duplicate combinations are not allowed. [Reported] [7][49]
    • Prices are floats like 2.40. How do you avoid floating-point equality bugs? [Reported] [17][78]
    • Now return only the count of combinations. Does order matter? [Reported] [10][48]
  5. Palindrome Pairs: find all pairs (i, j) such that words[i] + words[j] is a palindrome. [Reported] [5][7][39]
    • The interviewer wants O(nk) rather than O(nk^2). Which requirement do you clarify first? [Reported] [16]
    • How do the empty string and duplicate words affect correctness?
  6. IP range to CIDR: given a starting IPv4 address and a count n, return the shortest list of CIDR blocks that covers exactly that range. [Reported] [5][7][8]
    • How do you test the block boundaries, for example a range starting at an odd address?
  7. List-of-list (2D) iterator with hasNext, next and a remove that may be called once per next (LeetCode: Flatten 2D Vector; reported Oct 2025 as "Flatten 2D Array With Remove"). [Reported] [3][5][7][8]
    • What should remove do when it is called twice, or before next?
    • How would you make the iterator lazy over a very large nested input?
  8. Flatten Nested List Iterator. [Reported] [5][6]
  9. Cheapest flights / minimum cost with at most K stops. Return both the cost and the route. [Reported] [5][7][27]
    • If the route must be returned, which algorithms still work?
    • How would you handle ties, or a cycle of zero-cost edges?
  10. Sliding puzzle: find the minimum moves to solve the 2x3 board (LC 773), or produce a move sequence for the 3x3 (8-puzzle) variant. [Reported] [5][7][8]
    • How do you detect that a board is unsolvable before searching?
    • Would A* help, and with which heuristic?
  11. Terrain rendering plus water simulation: Part 1 prints the terrain as ASCII from a heights array. Part 2 drops water at a column and prints the terrain with W for water. The classic form is Pour Water (LC 755): pour V units at index K and return the final heights. [Reported] [3][5][7][35]
    • Which way should water flow when both sides are equally low?
    • How do you test the printed output exactly, including trailing whitespace?
  12. Round prices: round each price up or down so that the rounded values sum to the rounded total, minimizing the total rounding error (LC 1058). [Reported] [5][7][10]
    • What do you return when the target is unreachable?
  13. CSV parser: parse a CSV line with quoted fields, escaped quotes and commas inside quotes. [Reported] [7][8]
    • How do you handle a quoted field that spans multiple lines?
  14. Design File System: create(path, value) fails if the parent is missing, and get(path) returns the value. [Reported] [5][7]
    • How would you add delete with recursive children?
    • Add watch(path, callback), which fires when a create succeeds on that path or any path below it. [Reported] [81]
  15. Alien Dictionary: derive the letter order from a sorted word list. [Reported] [5][6][8]
    • How do you detect an invalid input, such as a longer word appearing before its own prefix?
  16. Regular expression matching with ., * and also +. [Reported] [5][7]
  17. Boggle game: find the path on a letter grid that spells the largest number of dictionary words (not just any word). Word Search II is the tagged LeetCode version. [Reported] [5][7]
  18. Pyramid transition matrix: can the bottom row be built up to a single root using the allowed triples? [Reported] [5][7][9]
  19. Ten wizards: the cost of a link is the squared difference of wizard IDs. Find the minimum-cost path from wizard 0 to wizard 9. [Reported] [7][8][9]
  20. Travel buddy list: friends whose wishlists overlap more than half with yours are "buddies". Recommend up to max cities from buddies' wishlists, ordered by similarity. [Reported] [7][57]
    • How do you break ties between buddies with equal overlap?
  21. Collatz conjecture: find the longest step count for any start value in [1, n]. [Reported] [3][7][65]
    • How would you memoize, and how do you bound memory for large n?
  22. Find the median of a file of integers too large for memory, with only sequential access. [Reported] [3][7][8]
  23. Guess the number: find a hidden 4-digit code (digits 1-6) using an API that returns how many digits are in the correct position. Minimize the number of API calls, and supply your own test cases. [Reported] [3][7][54][69]
    • Variant: talk to a server over a socket with a START/GUESS protocol. How do you structure the I/O? [Reported] [54]
  24. Meeting time / Employee Free Time: return the common free intervals across everyone's schedules. [Reported] [5][7]
  25. Maximum number of nights you can accommodate: choose non-adjacent booking requests to maximize nights (House Robber framing). [Reported] [5][7][9]
  26. Generate all upper- and lower-case combinations of a string and test each against a hidden string. [Reported] [7]
  27. K edit distance: find all dictionary words within edit distance k of a target. [Reported] [7][8]
    • How would a trie change the complexity?
  28. Preference list: merge everyone's ranked lists into one ordering that respects every person's order. [Reported] [7][8]
  29. Minimum vertices from which every node of a directed graph is reachable. [Reported] [3][5][7]
    • Does the graph contain cycles, and if so, how does that change the answer? [Reported] [16]
  30. Finding ocean (flood fill from a water cell), number of intersecting rectangles, and Hilbert curve step count. [Reported] [7][8][10]
  31. Implement a queue backed by a chain of fixed-size arrays. [Reported] [7][8]

B. Practical problems reported in 2025-2026 (current question bank)

  1. Split stay: given listings with lists of available day numbers and a date range, return every pair of listings [X, Y] where X covers a prefix of the range and Y covers the rest (one split only). [Reported] [3][18][19][20]
    • Your O(N^2 * D) solution works. The interviewer says something better exists. Where is the redundant work?
    • What if a split stay may use up to three listings?
    • How do you deduplicate symmetric pairs, and must the order be preserved?
    • Must the first listing cover a contiguous prefix and the second a contiguous suffix, or is plain set coverage enough? (The two readings give different answers on the reported example.) [Reported] [59]
    • One candidate wrote brute force with combinations and earned extra credit for a bitmask encoding of availability. How would you use one? [Reported] [59]
  2. Group lodging / select properties: for a target neighborhood and group size, choose properties with total capacity >= group size, minimizing the number of properties and then the total capacity. Return the IDs, or -1 or [] if impossible. [Reported] [4][21][41]
    • Which tie-break do you confirm first: fewer properties or less wasted capacity? [Reported] [21]
    • Is this subset-sum-like DP or backtracking, and what is the worst-case cost?
  3. Minimum-cost menu order: given single items and combos with prices plus a list of wanted items (at most 3 unique), return the lowest cost and the items to order. [Reported] [1][16][22][23]
    • You wrote DFS. Now convert it to DP. How do you add bitmask state? [Reported] [22]
    • Which menu entries can you prune before searching? [Reported] [16][56]
    • Return every optimal combination, not just one. [Reported] [16][62][63]
    • Variant II: order at least the required dish types using combos or singles. Why discard combos containing no required item before the DP? [Reported] [68]
    • Variant: rental "services" such as wifi, parking and TV instead of food. [Reported] [23]
  4. Menu bundles closest to budget: choose up to 3 bundles whose total is closest to, but not above, the budget. Write at least 5 test cases. [Reported] [35]
  5. Layover: the experience durations can each be booked repeatedly. Can you exactly fill the layover hours? Follow-up: what is the minimum number of bookings? [Reported] [1][3][51]
  6. Connect Four simulation: take a list of column moves, stop on a win or an invalid move, ignore the remaining moves, and print the grid. [Reported] [3][17][32][37][76]
    • How do you avoid rescanning the column for gravity on every move? [Reported] [32]
    • Check the diagonals without off-by-one errors in the limited time. [Reported] [16]
    • Determine win, draw or ongoing from a static board alone. [Reported] [3][73][74]
    • One interviewer asked for logically clear code over a clever algorithm, yet the candidate expected to fail for not running it. How do you budget 45 minutes so the diagonal check and a test run both fit? [Reported] [74]
  7. ConnectN object model: configurable width, height and N in a row to win, alternating players, and draw detection. [Reported] [15]
    • Which classes and interfaces would you expose so that a new rule, such as an irregular board, is easy to add?
  8. Retryer: wrap a function with maximum attempts, an interval, and exponential backoff (for example capped at 16 s). Rethrow the last error. Make it extensible. [Reported] [2][10][32][75][78]
    • How would you plug in jitter, retry-on-specific-exceptions or a time budget?
    • How do you unit-test timing without real sleeps?
  9. Key store with sum dependencies: set_value(k, v), set_sum(k, [keys]) (keys may repeat) and get_value(k). Updates must cascade to dependents. [Reported] [3][24][77][82]
    • What happens on a cycle?
    • Choose between eager propagation and lazy recompute for a workload with frequent writes and rare reads. [Reported] [2][16]
  10. Design Excel Sum Formula (LC 631). [Reported] [4][5]
  11. Banking class: deposit, withdraw, transfer, balance, and balance change over (start, end], where the boundary convention matters. [Reported] [2][3][34][62][63][64]
    • Your tests fail on a boundary. Which spec detail did you assume?
    • Online-assessment version: extend in levels to top spenders, scheduled payments and account merging, where later levels reuse earlier data structures. [Reported] [4]
  12. Refund allocation: given payments (method, date, amount) and existing refunds, allocate a new refund in full to one payment before the next. Prioritize by method (CREDIT, then CREDIT_CARD, then PAYPAL), and within a method by most recent payment. [Reported] [2][3][42]
    • How do earlier partial refunds change the remaining refundable amount per payment?
    • How do you design the data structures when the interviewer gives only examples? [Reported] [42]
  13. URL query parser: parse ?k=v&... into a map. Duplicate keys become lists, a bare key means true, and percent-encoded separators (%26, %3D) are decoded after splitting. [Reported] [2][3][26]
    • In a typed language, how do you return values that may be a string or a list?
    • Variant: a valueless key maps to an empty string rather than true. Which spec detail do you confirm first? [Reported] [2]
  14. Ski tournament: in a DAG of checkpoints with point values and edge travel times, find the path from start to any end node that maximizes points minus time, and print the path. [Reported] [3][17][42]
    • Is the input guaranteed acyclic? What if it is not?
    • Is Dijkstra correct here, and why or why not?
    • Extend to multiple skiers. How should the first version be structured so this follow-up is cheap? [Reported] [2][61]
  15. Task scheduling with deadlines: unit-time tasks with a deadline and a reward. Return the order that maximizes total reward (for example, (profit, deadline) = (100,2), (19,1), (27,2), (25,1), (15,3) gives 142). [Reported] [4][28][67][82]
    • Why does the naive greedy fail, and what is your optimal-runtime approach? [Reported] [28]
    • How do you find the latest free slot before a deadline quickly? [Reported] [2]
  16. Maximum Profit in Job Scheduling (LC 1235). Follow-up: output the chosen jobs. [Reported] [5][38]
  17. Account registration deduplication: accounts share email, phone or government ID. Return all duplicates of a given account. [Reported] [2][3][4]
    • Generalize from two attributes to an arbitrary attribute set. [Reported] [2]
    • What changes if new accounts stream in continuously?
  18. Build the smallest number from a list of digits (leading zeros dropped). Part 2: the smallest permutation that is >= a lower bound. [Reported] [2][30][55][64]
  19. Intersection of two linked lists where either list may contain a cycle (four structural cases). [Reported] [2][3][5]
  20. Multi-stream round-robin iterator: read bytes from several character streams in round-robin order with has_next and next. [Reported] [2][37]
  21. Calculate board score: for every cell, add the length of the same-character run going right and going down. R and C go up to 2000. [Reported] [3][53]
  22. Run-length encoding of an int array. Follow-ups: arithmetic-sequence RLE, and an iterator-based decoder for each encoding. [Reported] [25]
  23. Sum tree: leaves hold numbers and parents hold the sum of their children. Return any node's value, then support mutations (leaf to subtree, subtree to leaf) while keeping reads correct. [Reported] [29]
  24. Payment data store: insert(ts, value, id), get(id) and getValueInRange(start, end). [Reported] [33]
  25. Keyword tagging in reviews: wrap keywords as [tag]{keyword} case-insensitively. Overlapping matches go to the longer keyword. [Reported] [3][10]
  26. Replace text in a review using a replacement dictionary. [Reported] [3][10]
  27. Shortest uncommon substring: for each string, find the shortest substring that appears in no other string (LC 3076). [Reported] [4][5][31]
  28. Maximum Candies You Can Get from Boxes (LC 1298). It was given with no interface, so you define the API and write passing tests. [Reported] [5][14][36]
    • One solution passed every test but was rejected because its class model did not match the interviewer's intent. Which entities and methods would you model? [Reported] [2]
  29. Office layout grid (-1 wall, 0 open): find the maximum reachable open area and count the unreachable rooms. [Reported] [36]
  30. Two water jugs: return the minimum operations to measure a target volume and the sequence of states. [Reported] [10][27][64]
  31. Given three numbers, list every way the first two combine with + - * / to equal the third. Follow-up: allow parentheses. [Reported] [45][64]
  32. Card pile: by moving the top card to the bottom, can you reach N identical consecutive cards? [Reported] [44]
  33. Parse strings of letter-score tokens and compute per-letter totals. [Reported] [43]
  34. Find Median from Data Stream (LC 295). [Reported] [5][46]
  35. Chain booking: find the maximum number of cascading reservations. [Reported] [2][3]
  36. Compute browsing metrics in Python from event logs. [Reported] [3]
    • Which metrics would you compute per session versus per user, and how do you define a session?
  37. Durable key-value store serialization, an in-memory database with SQL operations, and an in-memory object manager with TTL. [Reported] [2][3]
  38. Car-speed controller: through a socket API (getStatus, setStatus(acceleration)), hold a simulated car at 29 mph. [Reported] [58] (The mirror files it under Airbnb; the page itself names no company.)

C. LeetCode Airbnb-tag problems (company tag; frequency per the mirrors)

  1. Smallest Sufficient Team (bitmask DP). It ranks near the top of the Airbnb tag frequency lists. [Reported] [4][5]
  2. Shopping Offers (DP and backtracking with bundles, the LeetCode cousin of the menu problem). [Reported] [5][16]

  10. Mini Parser and Basic Calculator II. [Reported] [5][6]

  12. Number of Ways to Build House of Cards. [Reported] [5][6]

C2. Additional practical problems reported 2025-2026 (added on review)

  1. Task build order: given tasks and prerequisite pairs (Course Schedule / Airflow-style DAG), decide whether every task can finish, then return a valid order. [Reported] [3][4][59][60]
    • One candidate mixed up in-degree and out-degree. Which test would have caught it? [Reported] [59]
    • How do you report which tasks are stuck in a cycle rather than just returning false?
  2. Two-hotel coverage with interval input: hotels are given as (start, end) availability ranges. Can at most two hotels cover a whole stay? The reported example marks (Apr 1-10), (Apr 5-15), (Apr 20-30) as covering Apr 5-25 (True), even though Apr 16-19 is uncovered. What would you ask the interviewer before coding? [Reported] [79]
    • Are dates nights or days? How does that change the boundary test?
  3. Maximum ski score on a height grid (n, m up to 100): each move goes to a strictly lower neighbor. Maximize the total height drop along a path. [Reported] [68]
    • Memoized DFS or processing cells in height order: which is easier to test in 45 minutes?
  4. Accommodation allocation: requests and segments each have a start and an end, and segments cannot overlap. Return the maximum number of fulfilled requests and the allocation itself. [Reported] [66]
    • The reporter passed with brute force. When does the input size force something better?
  5. Wallet class: create a wallet, deposit, withdraw and check the balance, with one wallet per user. A withdrawal that would overdraw must fail and leave the balance unchanged. No main or test harness is provided, so you write it. [Reported] [70]
    • Which invariants would you assert after every operation?
  6. Single-source distances in a weighted undirected graph (phone-screen problem-solving part). [Reported] [64]
    • The reporter proposed topological sort. Why does that not work here, and what does?
  7. Product of Array Except Self, without division and in O(n). [Reported] [72]
    • How do zeros in the input change your edge-case tests?
  8. Maximum Product Subarray. [Likely] [71] (The mirror files it under Airbnb, but the page has no interview context.)
  9. Search in a rotated sorted array, as a "classic with a twist" onsite problem. [Reported] [2]
    • How do duplicates break the O(log n) guarantee?
  10. Remove digits to form the smallest possible number (Remove K Digits). [Reported] [2]
  11. Print sentences inside a bordered, fixed-width table, wrapping words across lines. [Reported] [2]
    • What happens when one word is longer than the width?
  12. Gift-card spend: find the fewest items whose prices exactly equal the card value, and return the items. [Reported] [2][3]
    • What changes if each item can be bought at most once?
  13. Board-game region scoring: score every connected region of same-type cells on a grid. [Reported] [2]
  14. Maze score: given a board-game maze state, compute its score via shortest path. The reporter warns to expect an OOD angle, not just an algorithm. [Reported] [4][61]
  15. Infection spread time on a grid: return the time until every reachable cell is infected, or -1. [Reported] [2][3] - Follow-up: infection also spreads diagonally. How much of your BFS changes? [Reported] [2]
  16. Diplomacy battle resolution: parse <Army> <Location> <Hold | Support X | Move L> actions. Each army has strength 1 and each valid supporter adds 1. In a contested location the strongest army wins and ties kill all tied armies. A supporter whose own location is attacked loses its support. Return each army's final location or [dead]. [Reported] [7][80] - Can an army support a move into the location it is supporting from? Which ambiguities do you raise before coding?
  17. MLE phone screen: implement a key-value store, then state the test cases you would write against it. Onsite, extend it with additional constraints and show how the tests change. [Reported] [1]
  18. Document redaction exercise, reported in recent senior MLE loops: given text and a set of sensitive terms or patterns, return the redacted text. [Reported] [1] - How do you handle overlapping matches, case and word boundaries? Which tests prove the redaction is complete?
  19. Code review round: review about three pull requests in about 60 minutes and leave written comments. Reported planted defects include wrong results without a crash, swallowed exceptions, unchecked nulls, a race on shared state, missing error handling around an external API, and a hidden quadratic loop. [Reported] [2][4][59][60] - The third PR is long and time is short. How do you triage which comments to write first?
  20. Multi-level online assessment: cloud storage or file store with add, copy, prefix and suffix search, per-user quotas, and compression and decompression. The next level unlocks only when the current tests pass. [Reported] [3][4] - How do you choose level-1 data structures that will not break at level 4?
  21. Multi-level online assessment: task management system with add, update, get, search, priority sort, per-user quotas and an overdue report. [Reported] [4]

C3. SQL and data-analysis coding reported for Airbnb (relevant to experimentation roles)

  1. Find users with impressions on seven consecutive days. [Reported] [3] - How do you handle several impressions on the same day and time-zone boundaries?
  2. Find hosts who meet listing-count, rating and review-coverage rules. [Reported] [3]
  3. Estimate page-visit duration as the gap to the user's next event. [Reported] [3] - What do you do with the last event of a session, which has no next event?
  4. Calculate the median house price in each city in SQL. [Reported] [3]
  5. Compute the average vacant days per listing. [Reported] [3]
  6. Match rentals to required amenities (listings that have all of a given amenity set). [Reported] [3]
  7. Compute conversion (C/T) metrics from booking and visit tables. [Reported] [3] - Is the denominator visits, unique visitors or searches? How does the choice interact with the randomization unit?
  8. Build a user-by-period panel in SQL, then run a causal regression on it. [Reported] [3] - Which fixed effects would you include, and how do you cluster the standard errors?
  9. Model cumulative booking metrics with first and last booking times per user. [Reported] [3]

D. ML/experimentation-flavored practical coding (role-specific, not yet reported)

None of these were reported for Airbnb. They follow the same practical, "make it run with tests" format and fit an MLE role on experimentation, search ranking or pricing.

  1. Deterministic experiment bucketing: given user_id, an experiment salt and variant weights, return the assigned variant. [Likely] - How do you show that assignment is uniform and independent across two experiments? - How would you ramp from 5% to 50% without reassigning users already exposed?
  2. From raw exposure and conversion logs (user, experiment, variant, ts, event), compute per-variant conversion, the absolute and relative lift, and a 95% confidence interval. [Likely] - How do you handle a user exposed to both variants, and events that happen before exposure?
  3. Implement a sample-ratio-mismatch check for an expected 50/50 split and decide whether to alert. [Likely]
  4. Implement CUPED or regression adjustment on a per-user metric with a pre-period covariate, and report the variance reduction. [Likely]
  5. Implement a bootstrap confidence interval for the difference in a ratio metric (for example bookings per search). [Likely] - Why is resampling individual searches wrong when the unit of randomization is the user?
  6. Implement NDCG@k for ranked search results with graded relevance (booking > click > impression). [Likely] - How do you handle queries with no relevant results?
  7. Implement team-draft interleaving of two rankers' result lists and credit clicks to each ranker. [Likely]
  8. Re-rank a scored list of listings to enforce host diversity: at most one listing per host in each window of k, as in the pagination problem. [Likely] [52]
  9. Compute a rolling 7-day booking rate per listing from an event stream with bounded memory. [Likely]
  10. Given availability calendars and nightly prices, compute the total price of a stay, including weekly-discount and cleaning-fee rules, and test the boundary nights. [Likely]
  11. Reservoir-sample k events from a stream of unknown length, then make it weighted. [Likely]
  12. Implement streaming AUC, or approximate quantiles, over a large prediction log without loading it all in memory. [Likely]
  13. Write a sequential-testing guard: given daily cumulative results, decide whether a pre-registered stopping boundary has been crossed. [Likely]

E. Cross-cutting probes interviewers use in every coding round

  1. Before you code: what are the input format, the output format and the tie-breaking rules? Is any of them ambiguous? [Reported] [16][21]
  2. Write your own test cases now. Which edge cases did you choose, and why? [Reported] [1][2][35]
  3. What are the time and space complexity, and where is the bottleneck for the largest stated input? [Reported] [1][56]
  4. Your code fails one hidden test. How do you debug it in the remaining five minutes? [Likely] [17][34]
  5. A new requirement arrives, such as returning the path instead of the cost, or k columns instead of one. How much of your code has to change? [Reported] [3][4][27]
  6. If this ran in production behind an API endpoint, what would you validate, log or cache? [Likely] [18]
