03. ML System Design: Search Ranking, Embeddings, Pricing, Trust, Recommendations and More

Research note (read first). This chapter was reviewed again on 2026-10-14. The shared web-search budget was used up, and the network proxy could not resolve arxiv.org, dl.acm.org, medium.com, airbnb.tech, news.airbnb.com, kdd.org, glassdoor.com, teamblind.com, igotanoffer.com, interviewquery.com or trueinterview.io. Reddit refused the fetch. GitHub was the only reachable source. Four candidate-report pages were fetched and read there:

  • a TrueInterview-derived summary of the Airbnb MLE loop, built from 3 candidate reports (mostly 2026 Q1-Q2) [22];
  • a TrueInterview-derived Airbnb question index with report dates [23];
  • two translated 1point3acres (一亩三分地) Airbnb onsite posts that list the ML design rounds [24][25].

A general ML-system-design question list was also read [36].

Tagging policy:

  • [Reported] means one of those fetched pages names the prompt, or a close variant of it, for Airbnb. The TrueInterview question pages [26]-[35] are cited only as the links those pages give. They could not be opened directly.
  • [Reported, role unspecified] means the prompt is in the Airbnb index [23], but the page does not say whether it came from an MLE or a data-science loop.
  • [Likely] means the question is inferred from the role, the senior level and Airbnb's published systems. A paper or blog citation next to [Likely] points to the system the question probes. It does not mean anyone was asked the question.

The Airbnb papers and blog posts [1]-[12] and [16]-[21], [37] and [38] could not be re-fetched. They are listed from prior knowledge. Before the onsite, cross-check this list against Glassdoor, IGotAnOffer and LeetCode Discuss [13][14][15]. Those sites were unreachable for this review.

Format notes

  • Slot and length. The MLE-track summary says the loop is "filtered by design depth, not by code". It describes 1-2 ML system design rounds as the decisive rounds; the level guidance applies from Senior (G9) up [22].

    • One 1point3acres loop had two ML design rounds: guest/customer LTV and host pricing [24].
    • Another loop also had two: listing recommendation and price prediction. In the same loop, the "ML experience" round unexpectedly turned into a further ML design question [25].
    • Rounds run about 45-60 minutes over video, with a shared doc or whiteboard [22].

  • Interviewer. Usually a senior or staff MLE from an ML-heavy area such as Search/Relevance, Pricing, Trust, Experiences/Recommendations, Customer Support/GenAI or a platform team. Expect the interviewer to steer toward their own domain [Likely]. Recent senior loops reportedly added retrieval and ranking prompts around generative-AI customer-support tools [22].

  • Shape of the round. Typically one open-ended Airbnb product prompt, such as "design search ranking", "estimate a listing's lifetime value" or "detect relisted properties". You are expected to drive it:

    • Clarify the business goal and agree a definition of the target. In one LTV round the interviewer first made the candidate define "lifetime value" [22].
    • Pick the labels, design data and features, and choose a model.
    • Plan offline evaluation and online experimentation.
    • Cover serving and latency, then monitoring and iteration [22][1][2].

    Interviewers reportedly steer mid-round, for example from regression to bucketized classification in the LTV round, or into geohashing in the relisting round [22].

  • Senior bar. The round looks for these signals:

    • You frame the problem as a marketplace with two sides: guest and host, supply and demand.
    • You choose labels and objectives with care, including delayed or censored outcomes.
    • You propose a pragmatic baseline before reaching for deep models.
    • You reason about position bias, feedback loops and interference.
    • You design the launch experiment yourself.
    • You discuss trade-offs without heavy prompting.

    Airbnb's own search papers stress simple baselines, failed ideas and lessons learned [Likely; 1][2].

  • Experimentation weight. For this role, assume every design ends with "how would you launch and measure it?" Expect probes on interleaving, guardrails, two-sided interference and long-horizon metrics [Likely; 8][9][17]. Experiment-design prompts also appear in the Airbnb question index [23].

Questions

A. Search ranking for stays (core prompt)

  1. Design Airbnb search ranking, from candidate generation through offline and online evaluation and deployment: a guest enters a location, dates and guest count, and you return an ordered list of listings. [Reported] [22][26]
    • What is the business objective: bookings, booking value, guest satisfaction or long-term host and guest retention? How do you encode it?
    • What are the stages (candidate retrieval, filtering, first-pass ranker, second-pass re-ranker), and what is each stage's latency budget?
  2. What label would you train on, given that bookings are rare and many searches end with no booking? [Likely] [1]
    • How would you use clicks, listing-page views, wishlist saves, contacts to host and bookings as weaker labels or auxiliary tasks?
    • How do you treat a booking request the host later rejected or cancelled?
  3. How would you frame ranking: pointwise, pairwise or listwise? Why? [Likely] [1][2]
    • How would you build pairs from one search session with a single booked listing?
  4. How do you correct for position bias in logged search data? [Likely] [2]
    • How would you model position as a feature during training, and what do you set it to at inference time?
    • How would you validate that your debiasing helped, rather than just shifting the distribution?
  5. Which feature families would you build for listings, queries, guests and query-listing pairs? [Likely] [1]
    • How do you normalize price so that the model generalizes across markets and currencies?
    • How do you represent location relevance (distance to map center, the neighborhood the guest wanted)?
  6. Your first model was a GBDT. When would you move to a neural network, and what would you expect to break? [Likely] [1]
    • How do you handle feature scaling, sparse categorical features and very wide value ranges in a neural ranker?
  7. The ranker strongly prefers cheaper listings. Is that a bug or correct behavior? How would you investigate? [Likely] [1][2]
    • How would you make the model price-aware without hard-coding a monotonic price penalty?
  8. How do you handle cold-start listings that have no booking or review history? [Likely] [2]
    • How do you estimate engagement for a new listing, and how do you give it controlled exposure without hurting conversion?
  9. How would you add diversity to the results so the top of the page is not ten near-identical listings? [Likely] [5]
    • How would you measure diversity, and how would you trade it off against relevance in the loss?
  10. How would you rank listings shown as map pins, where there is no clear top-to-bottom position? [Likely] [2][38]
    • How does user attention on a map differ from attention on a list, and how does that change your discounting?
  11. How would you model the whole guest search journey (many searches across days before one booking) rather than a single search? [Likely] [6]
    • What would a multi-task model with heads for clicks, long views, wishlist and booking look like? How do you weight the heads?
  12. How do you handle flexible-date or flexible-location searches ("I'm flexible", category browsing)? [Likely] [11]
  13. How would you incorporate host-side outcomes (acceptance, cancellations, host earnings) into guest-facing ranking? [Likely] [1]
  14. Offline NDCG went up but the online A/B test showed flat bookings. Walk me through your debugging plan. [Likely] [1][2]
    • How would you check for training-serving skew, feature leakage and a mismatch between offline and online candidate sets?
  15. What offline evaluation set would you build, and how do you avoid leakage from future data or from repeat sessions by the same guest? [Likely] [1]
  16. Rank home-page search results when the guest has typed no text query, for example on app open or a logged-in home feed. [Reported] [23][29]
    • What signals replace the query: past searches, wishlists, location, season, booking history?
    • How do you handle a brand-new guest with no history?
    • How does this ranking differ from query-driven ranking in objective and evaluation?
  17. A guest types a natural-language query ("cabin near a lake with a hot tub for six next weekend"). How would you parse it into hard filters, soft preferences and a semantic retrieval query? [Likely] [12]
    • How do you evaluate parsing errors that silently drop a hard constraint?
    • Where would you use an LLM, and how would you bound its latency and cost on the search path?

B. Candidate retrieval and serving infrastructure for search

  1. Design candidate retrieval that cuts millions of listings to a few thousand within strict latency, respecting date availability, guest count and map bounds. [Likely] [12]
    • Where would embedding-based retrieval fit next to hard filters, and how do you combine them?
  2. How would you train a two-tower model for embedding-based retrieval of listings? [Likely] [12]
    • What is your negative-sampling strategy (in-batch, same-market, hard negatives)?
    • How do you handle availability, which changes daily and cannot easily live inside the embedding?
  3. Design semantic search driven by user embeddings: represent each guest by an embedding and retrieve listings or destinations that match their intent. [Reported] [23][31]
    • How do you build and refresh the user embedding from sparse, long-gap travel histories?
    • How do you serve approximate nearest-neighbor search under geography and availability filters?
    • How do you evaluate retrieval recall separately from end-to-end ranking quality?
  4. How would you serve a neural ranker at p99 latency under 200 ms for thousands of candidates per query? [Likely]
    • Which features are precomputed offline, which are computed in real time, and how do you keep them consistent?
  5. How do you prevent training-serving skew in features such as "listing price for these dates" or "number of recent bookings"? [Likely] [1]
  6. How often would you retrain the ranking model, and how would you detect that it has gone stale? [Likely]
  7. What would you log at serving time so you can retrain, debias and debug later? [Likely] [2]
  8. Listings with stale calendars cause booking requests that hosts then decline. How would you predict that a listing's availability is unreliable, and use that in retrieval or ranking? [Likely] [1]

C. Listing embeddings and real-time personalization

  1. Design listing embeddings trained from guest click sessions, and use them for similar-listing recommendations and in-session personalization. [Likely] [3][10]
    • How do you define a session and its context window?
    • What negatives would you use? Why might random negatives make embeddings that only learn geography?
  2. How would you change the embedding objective so that the booked listing influences every listing in the session? [Likely] [3]
  3. How would you build user-type and listing-type embeddings to capture long-term preferences from sparse booking histories? [Likely] [3]
  4. How would you create an embedding for a brand-new listing with no sessions? [Likely] [3]
  5. How would you evaluate embeddings offline before using them in production? [Likely] [3]
    • What would a "rank of the eventually booked listing among the clicked listings" evaluation look like?
  6. How would you turn in-session clicks and skips into real-time ranking features? [Likely] [3]
    • What streaming infrastructure is needed, and what is the freshness requirement?
  7. When would you replace skip-gram embeddings with a sequence model or transformer over user actions? What would you expect to gain? [Likely]

D. Pricing, price prediction and lifetime value

  1. Design a system that helps hosts set prices for each future date, taking market demand into account. [Reported] [24][25]
    • What is the objective: booking probability, expected host revenue, or marketplace occupancy?
    • Why can't you train directly on "the optimal price" as a label?
  2. Predict the nightly price a listing should command (price prediction), including for a brand-new listing with no booking history. [Reported] [25]
    • Which comparable-listing, location, amenity and seasonality features would you use?
    • How do you avoid learning hosts' mispricing as the target?
  3. How would you predict booking probability as a function of price, lead time and listing quality? [Likely] [4]
    • How do you handle price being confounded with demand, because hosts raise prices when they expect demand?
  4. How would you estimate price elasticity per listing or per market from observational data? [Likely] [4]
    • When would you need randomized price experiments, and what ethical and host-trust constraints apply?
  5. Hosts can accept, modify or ignore your suggestion. How do you evaluate the model offline and online? [Likely] [4]
    • What is your unit of randomization, and how do you handle spillover between treated and control hosts in the same market?
  6. How would you forecast market demand for a city on a given date (events, holidays, seasonality)? [Likely] [16]
  7. How would you prevent the pricing model from producing extreme or volatile suggestions that destroy host trust? [Likely]
  8. How would you design total-price display or fee-related ranking changes, and how would you measure whether guests trust the price more? [Likely]
  9. A pricing-model launch raised bookings but cut average host revenue. How do you decide? [Likely] [17]
  10. Estimate the lifetime value of a property (listing). First agree with the interviewer on what lifetime value means here. [Reported] [22][27]
    • What horizon, which revenue (gross booking value or Airbnb take), and how do you treat churned or delisted hosts?
    • The interviewer pushes you from regression to bucketized classification. Why might value buckets work better for heavy-tailed, censored LTV, and what do you lose? [Reported] [22]
    • How would the LTV estimate be used, for example in host acquisition, support prioritization or ranking?
  11. Design a system that computes a guest's (customer's) lifetime value on the platform. [Reported] [24]
    • How do you handle guests who book once every two years, so that the data is censored and very sparse?
    • How would you validate an LTV model whose labels take years to mature?
  12. Which hosts should be prompted to turn on Smart Pricing or adopt a suggested price? Design an uplift (heterogeneous treatment-effect) model and the experiment that validates it. [Likely] [4][17]

E. Fraud, risk and trust and safety

  1. Design a model that scores every booking for payment-fraud risk in real time. [Likely] [18]
    • Chargeback labels arrive weeks later. How do you train and evaluate with delayed labels?
    • How do you handle label censoring, where blocked transactions never reveal whether they were fraud?
  2. Rather than block or allow, how would you design graduated friction (ID verification, extra payment checks) driven by risk scores? [Likely] [18]
    • How do you choose thresholds per action, given the cost of false positives (lost good guests) and false negatives?
  3. Design an account-takeover detection system for guest and host logins. [Likely] [18]
  4. Design a system that predicts and prevents unauthorized parties at listings. [Likely] [19]
    • What features are legitimate and fair to use, and which risk proxy discrimination (for example by age)?
    • How do you measure the system's impact when the outcome (a party happened) is rarely observed?
  5. Design a fake or scam listing detector at listing-creation time. [Likely] [19]
    • How do you combine text, image, host-history and graph signals (shared devices, payment instruments)?
  6. Design a relisting-detection system: find a property that was removed or banned and then relisted, possibly under a new account. [Reported] [22][28]
    • How does geohashing help you generate candidate pairs efficiently? [Reported] [22]
    • How would the outputs show up in online metrics, and what would you watch for false-positive harm to legitimate hosts? [Reported] [22]
    • How would you separate relisting from legitimate cases such as a new host buying the property, or a co-host managing it?
  7. Design a system that detects duplicate listings (the same property listed several times at the same time). [Likely]
  8. Design a model that detects attempts to move payment or communication off the platform in guest-host messages. [Likely] [19][20]
  9. How would you use graph-based features or graph neural networks to detect fraud rings? [Likely]
  10. Fraudsters adapt to your model within weeks. How do you design for adversarial drift? [Likely]
    • How do you keep a clean holdout to measure true fraud rates when the model blocks most of it?
  11. How do you A/B test a fraud model when exposing the control group to fraud has real costs? [Likely] [18]
  12. How do you build fairness checks into a trust model that affects who can book? [Likely] [19]

F. Recommendations (Experiences, similar listings, discovery surfaces)

  1. Design Airbnb listing recommendation for a guest. [Reported] [25][36]
    • What are the candidate sources (embedding neighbors, popular-in-destination, wishlist-similar), and how do you blend them?
    • How does it differ from search ranking when the guest has no dates or destination yet?
  2. Design search ranking for Airbnb Experiences, which has far less data than stays. [Likely] [7]
    • How would you stage the system as data grows (heuristics, then GBDT, then personalization, then online scoring)?
  3. Design "similar listings" recommendations on the listing detail page. [Likely] [3]
    • What is the right objective: clicks, bookings, or rescuing guests whose first choice is unavailable?
  4. Design personalized marketing or email recommendations (destinations, wishlisted listings with price drops). [Likely]
    • How do you measure incremental lift rather than attribution from people who would have booked anyway?
  5. Design recommendations for cross-selling Experiences or Services to guests with an upcoming stay. [Likely]
  6. Homes, Experiences and Services now share discovery surfaces. How would you rank a blended feed whose item types have different conversion rates, prices and data volumes? [Likely] [7][37]
    • How do you calibrate scores across verticals so that one vertical does not crowd out the others?
  7. How would you recommend wishlist listings or destinations for group trips where several people collaborate? [Likely]
  8. Design a system that matches hosts with co-hosts or local service providers. [Likely] [37]
    • What does a successful match mean, and how do you handle two-sided preferences and capacity limits?
  9. How do you avoid a popularity feedback loop in which top listings get more exposure, more bookings and more exposure? [Likely] [5]

G. Host acceptance, response time and other time-to-event predictions

  1. Design a model that predicts the probability a host will accept a booking request, and use it in ranking. [Likely] [1]
    • How do you avoid unfairly penalizing a host for a single rejection?
    • How do you use host preferences (trip length, lead time, gaps between bookings) as features?
  2. Design a model that predicts host response time to a guest message or booking request. [Likely]
    • How do you handle censoring for messages that never get a reply?
    • Would you use survival or hazard models, or classification at fixed horizons? Why?
  3. Predict the probability that a confirmed booking will be cancelled by the host or by the guest. [Likely]
    • How would you use this in ranking, in host coaching, or in guest-protection policy?
  4. Predict booking lead time and length of stay for a search, and explain how they would change ranking. [Likely] [1]
  5. Design a model that estimates customer-support wait time or resolution time, and routes cases to the right agent queue. [Likely] [20]

H. Categories, content understanding and listing quality

  1. Design the system behind Airbnb Categories (for example "Beachfront", "Treehouses"), which assigns listings to themed browse categories. [Likely] [11]
    • How do you combine ML candidates with human-in-the-loop review, and how do you budget human review?
    • How do you rank listings within a category, and how do you rank the categories themselves for a user?
  2. Design an image-understanding pipeline that detects amenities and room types from listing photos. [Likely] [21]
    • How would you get labels cheaply, and how do you measure precision when amenities drive search filters?
  3. Choose a listing's cover photo to raise its booking rate. [Reported] [22]
    • How do you handle cold start for a new listing with no engagement on any photo? [Reported] [22]
    • How do you avoid choosing a photo that raises clicks but misleads guests, and so raises cancellations or bad reviews?
    • Would you treat this as a bandit, a learned photo-quality model, or both?
  4. Design a model that organizes a listing's photos into a room-by-room tour and orders them. [Likely] [21][37]
  5. Design a system for finding high-quality listings, used in ranking and host coaching. [Reported] [22]
    • How do you define quality without simply rewarding listings that are already popular?
    • Which signals would you use: photos, description, completeness, reviews, cancellations, response rate?
  6. Design the selection of a "most-loved" or "Guest Favorite" designation from ratings, reviews and reliability. [Likely] [37]
    • How do you keep the badge stable over time and hard to game, and how do you measure its effect on bookings?
  7. Design a system that detects inaccurate or misleading listing descriptions by comparing text, photos and reviews. [Likely]
  8. How would you use LLMs or multimodal models for listing understanding at Airbnb's scale while controlling cost? [Likely]
  9. How would you translate or localize listing content and decide when machine translation is good enough to show? [Likely]

I. Reviews: quality, ranking and trust

  1. Design a system that ranks reviews on a listing page so guests see the most helpful reviews first. [Likely]
    • How do you define "helpful" without explicit helpfulness votes?
  2. Design a fake or retaliatory review detector. [Likely] [19]
  3. Design a system that extracts aspects (cleanliness, location, accuracy) from review text and feeds them into search or listing quality. [Likely]
  4. Design LLM-generated review summaries or highlights for a listing. [Likely]
    • How do you evaluate faithfulness, so the summary claims nothing the reviews do not support?
    • How do you A/B test whether summaries change booking or cancellation behavior?
  5. Ratings are highly skewed toward 5 stars. How do you build a reliable quality signal from them? [Likely]
    • How would you apply Bayesian shrinkage for listings with few reviews?
  6. How would you predict a guest's post-stay satisfaction before the stay, and how would you use it in ranking? [Likely] [1]

J. Messaging, customer support and LLM systems

  1. Generate customer-support instructions (step-by-step agent guidance) from Airbnb's company policies. [Reported] [22][30]
    • How do you keep generated instructions faithful to the source policy when policies change?
    • How do you evaluate correctness before agents or an automated assistant act on them?
  2. Design the retrieval and ranking layer for a generative-AI customer-support tool: given a case, retrieve the right policies, past resolutions and booking context. [Reported] [22]
    • What are the candidate sources, and what is the relevance label for training a ranker?
    • How do you measure whether better retrieval actually reduces resolution time or escalations?
  3. Design an ML system that classifies the intent of guest-host messages to route or auto-suggest replies. [Likely] [20]
  4. Design an LLM-based customer-support assistant that resolves common issues (cancellations, refunds) and escalates the rest. [Likely] [20]
    • How do you evaluate it offline, monitor hallucinations and policy errors, and measure deflection versus satisfaction online?
    • What guardrails are needed for actions involving money or safety?
  5. Design suggested replies for hosts. How do you avoid generating replies that commit the host to things they did not intend? [Likely]
  6. How would you build an LLM-as-judge pipeline to produce relevance or quality labels for search and support, and how would you validate it against human raters? [Likely] [20]

K. Experimentation and measurement inside the ML design round

  1. For your ranking model, what is the primary metric of the launch A/B test, and what are the guardrails? [Likely] [8][17]
    • Why might "bookings" be a better primary metric than "booking requests" or "clicks"?
  2. Design robust primary and guardrail metrics for a product or model change, so the metrics resist gaming and noise. [Reported, role unspecified] [23][33]
  3. What randomization unit would you use for a search ranking test (guest, session, search, listing, market)? Why? [Likely] [8]
  4. Explain why a guest-randomized test of a ranking change can be biased in a two-sided marketplace with limited inventory. [Likely] [8]
    • How could market-level, cluster or switchback designs help? What do they cost in statistical power?
  5. Design an experiment for a listing badge (for example a "fast Wi-Fi" badge) when guests and listings interact across a network and treatment can spill over. [Reported, role unspecified] [23][35]
  6. Design an A/B test with causal inference for a case where clean randomization is hard (hosts opt in, or the change is market-wide). [Reported, role unspecified] [23][34]
    • Which quasi-experimental methods would you use (difference-in-differences, synthetic control, regression discontinuity), and how do you check their assumptions?
  7. When would you use interleaving instead of a classic A/B test for ranking? [Likely] [9]
    • How do you attribute a booking to one ranker in an interleaved result list?
    • How do you validate that interleaving and A/B testing agree on direction?
  8. Bookings happen days after search, and stays happen weeks later. How do you get a sensitive, timely decision metric? [Likely] [8][17]
    • How would you build and validate a surrogate or predicted long-term value metric?
  9. How would you reduce variance in a booking-conversion experiment (CUPED, stratification, trimming)? [Likely] [8]
  10. How do you detect and handle sample ratio mismatch, bot traffic or logging bugs in a ranking experiment? [Likely] [8]
  11. How do you handle novelty and primacy effects, and seasonality around holidays, in experiment readouts? [Likely]
  12. Your model wins overall but loses badly in one market or one guest segment. What do you do? [Likely]
  13. How would you set up a long-term holdout to measure the cumulative impact of many ranking launches? [Likely]
  14. How would you measure the impact of a pricing or fraud model where the treatment changes host or fraudster behavior over time? [Likely] [4][18]
  15. How would you use counterfactual or off-policy evaluation from logged ranking data before running an online test? [Likely] [2] - What propensities do you need to log, and how much randomization would you inject?
  16. How do you trade off guest-side and host-side metrics in a single ship decision? [Likely] [17]
  17. Design the experimentation platform that your ML launches run on: assignment, exposure logging, metric computation and readout. [Reported, role unspecified] [23][32] - How would it support marketplace designs (market-level or switchback) and interleaving, not only user-level tests?
  18. The ranking team has more candidate models than traffic to test them. How would you prioritize, run sequential or adaptive tests, and control false discoveries across many launches? [Likely] [9]

L. Monitoring, reliability and iteration (senior depth)

  1. What would you monitor once your ranking or fraud model is live (data drift, feature nulls, score distributions, business KPIs)? [Likely] - Which alerts would page an on-call engineer, and which go to a dashboard?
  2. How would you roll out a new model safely (shadow scoring, canary, ramp, automatic rollback)? [Likely]
  3. A feature pipeline broke silently and the model has served on stale features for three days. How do you detect it, mitigate it and prevent it from happening again? [Likely]
  4. How do you decide what to build next after launch: more features, a bigger model, or better labels? [Likely] [1][2]
  5. How would you reduce serving cost by 50% without measurable loss in bookings? [Likely] - What is your approach to distillation, caching and candidate-set pruning?
  6. How would you design the system so that other teams (Experiences, Services, Long-term stays) can reuse your ranking stack? [Likely]
  7. Which parts of this design would you not use ML for at all, and why? [Likely] [1]
  8. What are the main fairness and discrimination risks in Airbnb ranking and trust systems, and how would you audit for them? [Likely] [19]

M. Variations and curveball prompts

  1. Design ranking for long-term stays (28+ nights). What changes from nightly stays? [Likely]
  2. Design ranking for Airbnb Services or new verticals with almost no historical data. [Likely] [7][37]
  3. Design autocomplete and query understanding for the search box (locations, landmarks, categories). [Likely]
  4. Design a model that predicts which markets will face supply shortages for an upcoming event, and recommend actions to recruit hosts. [Likely]
  5. Design a host-onboarding recommendation system that tells new hosts what to improve first. [Likely]
  6. Design a "price drop" or "rare find" badge. How would you verify that badges are accurate and actually help guests? [Likely]
  7. The interviewer scheduled a project deep dive but instead gives you an unfamiliar ML design prompt. How do you scope it in the first five minutes? [Reported] [25]

04. A/B Testing and Experimentation Deep Dive: ERF, Marketplace Interference, Variance Reduction, Interleaving and Sequential Testing

Research note (read first). This chapter went through an adversarial review on 2026-10-14. During that review the egress proxy blocked medium.com, arxiv.org, igotanoffer.com, glassdoor.com, dl.acm.org, airbnb.tech, teamblind.com, 1point3acres.com, leetcode.com and reddit.com, and the shared web-search budget was already used up. Only raw.githubusercontent.com was reachable. Five GitHub-hosted sources were fetched and read: two curated indexes of company engineering posts [23][24], which confirm the title, URL and year of the Airbnb posts cited here; the community Airbnb question list [22]; and two general ML/DS interview question banks. None of them contains a candidate report of an Airbnb experimentation or A/B-testing question. Every question is therefore tagged [Likely]. No question is tagged [Reported], and none could honestly be. The 2024-2026 candidate reports the review set out to find (Glassdoor, Blind, 1point3acres, LeetCode Discuss) could not be reached. Citations such as "[Likely; 3]" point to the publication that makes a topic a plausible probe. They do not mean the question was asked. The References section marks each entry as confirmed via index (title, URL and year confirmed from [23] or [24], page itself not opened), verified (page fetched and read) or unverified (from prior knowledge only). To close the gap, rerun with web access and look for real candidate reports.

Format notes

  • Where this material shows up. Public descriptions of Airbnb's engineering loop (coding x2, system design, ML design, past-project deep dive, core values x2) do not list a separate "A/B testing" round for MLEs [9][10]. For an MLE role that names experimentation or A/B testing, expect it in three places: (a) the evaluation and launch half of the ML design round; (b) the past-project deep dive, with questions like "how did you know it worked?"; and (c) possibly a dedicated experimentation or analytics conversation with a data scientist or experimentation-platform engineer. Ask your recruiter which of these your loop includes. [Likely]

  • Duration and interviewer. Usually 45-60 minutes with a senior or staff MLE, a data scientist (Airbnb's DS org has an Inference/Experimentation track) or an engineer from the experimentation platform team (ERF) [1][3]. [Likely]

  • The senior (L5) bar. It goes beyond knowing the t-test. Expect to show you can:

    • spot when the standard A/B setup is biased (interference, novelty, selection, peeking);
    • choose the randomization unit and the metrics, and defend that choice;
    • quantify trade-offs between bias and variance and between speed and rigor;
    • turn ambiguous or conflicting results into a launch decision that Product and leadership accept.

    Airbnb's own writing stresses that experiments run on a two-sided marketplace with long, rare conversion funnels (search, then contact or book, then stay, then review). Expect probes on exactly that [1][2][4][5]. [Likely]

  • Format of the questions. Expect open-ended scenarios ("we changed X in search ranking; design the test; results came back like this; what now?"). Some rounds may add light whiteboard math, such as sample size, standard errors or CUPED adjustment. Expect few closed-form trivia questions. [Likely]

Questions

A. Airbnb experimentation platform (ERF) and experiment fundamentals

  1. Walk me through how you would design an online A/B test for a new search-ranking model at Airbnb, from hypothesis to launch decision. [Likely; 1][2]
    • What is your primary metric, and why not just click-through rate?
    • What is the randomization unit, and what are you assuming by choosing it?
    • How long would you run it, and what determines that?
  2. Airbnb's experimentation platform (ERF, the Experiment Reporting Framework) computes metrics for hundreds of concurrent experiments. If you were designing such a platform, what are its core components? [Likely; 3]
    • How would you implement deterministic, salted hashing for assignment so that concurrent experiments are independent?
    • How do you log exposures versus assignments, and why does the difference matter?
    • How would you make metric definitions consistent across teams (for example, through a central metrics layer)? [Likely; 11]
  3. Airbnb's 2017 post is about scaling ERF as the number of experiments grew. If concurrent experiments and metrics grew 10x again, where would the pipeline break first, and how would you restructure it? [Likely; 3]
    • What would you precompute per user-day versus compute on demand?
    • How would you keep results reproducible when metric definitions change mid-experiment?
  4. What is the difference between assignment and exposure (trigger) analysis, and when is an intent-to-treat analysis the right choice? [Likely]
    • The treatment only changes the page when a listing has more than 10 photos. Who should be in your analysis population?
    • How does triggering affect your power, and how do you translate a triggered effect back to a site-wide impact?
  5. Your exposure log fires on page load for every visitor, including those who never reach the component you changed (overtracking). How does this dilute the measured effect and the power, and how would you fix both the logging and the analysis? [Likely; 29]
  6. For a search-ranking test, treatment and control produce identical results on many queries. How would you define a triggered population of queries where the rankings actually differ, and what does computing the counterfactual control ranking cost in serving? [Likely; 29][8]
    • How do you avoid bias when the trigger condition itself depends on the treatment model's output?
  7. Many Airbnb users browse logged out and on several devices before booking. How does this affect randomization and metric attribution? [Likely; 1]
    • Would you randomize by cookie, device, user ID or something else? What breaks in each case?
    • How would you handle a user who is assigned on a logged-out device and then logs in?
  8. Request-to-book listings need host acceptance, and Instant Book listings do not. How do you define 'conversion' for a guest-side experiment when part of the outcome is the host's decision? [Likely]
    • What happens to your metric if treatment sends more requests to hosts with low acceptance rates?
  9. What is a sample ratio mismatch (SRM)? How would you detect it, and what are its common causes on a web/mobile booking site? [Likely]
    • The treatment loads more slowly, so fewer treatment users fire the exposure log. What does that do to your results?
    • Would you ever analyze an experiment that fails the SRM check?
  10. How would you run an A/A test, and what would you use it for on a mature platform? [Likely]
    • Your A/A tests show 9% significant results at alpha = 0.05 on one metric. What do you investigate?
  11. Explain the p-value and the confidence interval of a difference in means to a product manager who wants a yes/no answer. [Likely]
  12. How do you compute the sample size and minimum detectable effect (MDE) for a booking-conversion experiment? [Likely; 1]
    • Booking conversion is low and bookings are lumpy. How does that change the calculation?
    • The PM wants a two-week answer, but power analysis says six weeks. What options do you propose?
  13. Your metric is a ratio, such as bookings per search or revenue per booking, where the analysis unit differs from the randomization unit. How do you compute its variance correctly? [Likely]
    • When would you use the delta method, and when a bootstrap?
    • What goes wrong if you treat each search as independent?
  14. How do you handle heavy-tailed metrics like gross booking value, where a few long or luxury stays dominate? [Likely]
    • Would you winsorize/cap, log-transform or use a different estimator? What does each do to interpretation?
  15. A treatment raises bookings per user but lowers booking conversion per search. How can both be true, and which metric do you trust for the launch decision? [Likely]
  16. Airbnb's 2014 post describes an experiment whose result flipped depending on when it was checked, and a case where a browser-specific bug drove the result. What general lessons do you take from such cases? [Likely; 1]
    • How would you build a platform guard against "result depends on the day you looked"?
    • How would you segment by platform/browser without going on a fishing expedition?
  17. How long should a booking experiment run given weekly seasonality and a lead time between search and check-in of days to months? [Likely; 1]
    • What do you lose if you stop after full weeks but before guests actually stay?
  18. How would you design guardrails that block a launch automatically? What would the guardrail metrics be for a search change at Airbnb? [Likely; 4]
    • How do you set the guardrail threshold so it is neither too noisy nor too lax?
    • Who should be able to override a guardrail, and how should overrides be logged?
  19. Airbnb has written about designing experimentation guardrails. Beyond a guardrail that fires on a large negative impact, why might a platform also need a guardrail that checks whether each guardrail metric is adequately powered, and how does it change how long teams must run tests? [Likely; 4]

B. Metric choice: north stars, proxies and long-term value

  1. What should the primary success metric be for a search-ranking experiment at Airbnb: bookings, booking value, nights booked, uncancelled bookings or something else? Defend your choice. [Likely; 2][5]
    • Why might "uncancelled bookings" or "booking with no host cancellation" be better than raw bookings?
    • How would you weigh guest-side gains against host-side outcomes?
  2. Bookings are slow and rare, so the team wants a faster proxy metric (listing clicks, wishlist saves, contacts). How do you validate that a proxy is aligned with the north star? [Likely; 2]
    • Show me a scenario where the proxy improves and bookings drop.
    • How would you use historical experiments to measure proxy-to-north-star correlation, and what are the pitfalls?
  3. Airbnb has written about putting a monetary future value on actions so that teams can compare trade-offs. How would you use such a metric in an experiment, and how could it mislead you? [Likely; 6]
    • How do you validate a model-based "future value" metric before letting it decide launches?
  4. How would you measure long-term effects such as repeat booking, host churn or review quality that appear months after a test ends? [Likely]
    • What are the trade-offs of long-running holdouts?
    • How would you use surrogate indices or a long-term holdback group?
    • After launch, how would you use a long-term holdback to measure whether the feature's effect decays? How large should it be and how long should it run? [Likely]
  5. Design an overall evaluation criterion (OEC) for a pricing-suggestion feature shown to hosts. [Likely; 7]
    • What are the host-side metrics, the guest-side metrics and the marketplace-level metrics?
    • Host earnings rise but occupancy falls. Is that a win?
  6. How do you choose between a mean metric (average bookings per user) and a proportion metric (percentage of users who book)? [Likely]
  7. How would you test whether a change shifts the 90th percentile of a metric, such as search latency or booking lead time, rather than the mean? How do you get valid confidence intervals for a quantile difference at platform scale? [Likely; 30]
  8. You track 50 metrics, and three moved significantly in the wrong direction. How do you decide whether to worry? [Likely]
    • How do you control for multiple comparisons without destroying power?
    • Which metrics would you pre-register as primary, secondary and guardrail?
  9. How would you measure the quality of search results beyond conversion, for example relevance, diversity or "guest found what they wanted"? [Likely; 8]
    • How would you test a diversity objective whose value is not captured by short-term bookings?
  10. Airbnb has published work on managing diversity in search and on learning to rank diversely. How would you run an experiment on a diversity change whose benefit is that some guests find a match they would otherwise miss, while per-search booking rate for the majority may dip slightly? [Likely; 25][26]
    • Which metric captures 'guests who would otherwise have booked nothing'?
  11. Cancellation and refund outcomes happen after the experiment window closes. How do you build a metric that accounts for them without waiting months? [Likely]

C. Marketplace interference and two-sided experiments

  1. Why can a standard user-randomized A/B test give a biased estimate in a two-sided marketplace like Airbnb? Explain it with an example. [Likely; 12][13]
    • In which direction is the bias usually, for a ranking change that helps treatment guests book popular listings?
    • When is the interference negligible?
  2. A treatment ranking model steers guests toward high-quality listings. Treatment and control guests compete for the same limited inventory on the same dates. What does the A/B test overstate, and why? [Likely; 12]
    • How would you estimate the size of this cannibalization bias?
  3. Compare guest-side (demand) randomization with listing/host-side (supply) randomization. When would you use each? [Likely; 13]
    • Which side do you randomize for a host pricing tool, and what interference does that leave?
    • What is the interference pattern when you randomize listings for a ranking boost?
  4. Explain two-sided randomization (randomizing both guests and listings, then analyzing the cells). What bias does it reduce, and at what cost? [Likely; 13]
  5. Write down the estimand you actually care about for a marketplace launch (the global treatment effect: everyone treated versus no one treated). How does it differ from the difference in means in a user-randomized test under interference? [Likely; 13]
  6. Airbnb collaborated on research about reducing interference bias in marketplace pricing experiments. How would you design a pricing experiment that limits interference? [Likely; 12]
    • How would cluster randomization by market or by "similar listings" help, and what does it do to variance?
    • How would you build listing clusters (for example, by geography and listing similarity) so that most competition stays within a cluster?
  7. Compare switchback (time-based) designs, geo/market-level randomization and cluster randomization for an Airbnb search or pricing change. [Likely]
    • Why are switchbacks awkward when there is a long lead time between booking and stay?
    • With only a few hundred markets, how do you get enough power from a market-level experiment?
  8. How would you detect interference empirically, for example by running the same treatment at two allocation levels (10% versus 50%)? [Likely; 13]
    • What would you conclude if the effect at 50% allocation is half the effect at 10%?
  9. Describe an 'A/B test of A/B tests': randomly assigning clusters to either individual-level or cluster-level randomization and comparing the two estimates to detect interference. How would you adapt this for Airbnb listings clustered by market and date? [Likely; 27]
    • How many clusters would you need for the comparison to have any power?
  10. Market conditions matter. When supply is tight (peak dates), interference bias grows. How would you account for supply/demand balance in the analysis? [Likely; 13]
    • Would you report the effect separately for supply-constrained and demand-constrained markets?
  11. A change makes guests book earlier. Total bookings over the quarter may not change, but the A/B test shows a lift. How would you detect this kind of intertemporal substitution (pull-forward)? [Likely]
  12. A host-side change (for example, better calendar sync) increases the number of available nights. How would you measure its marketplace effect, given that extra supply in treatment can take bookings from control listings? [Likely]
  13. How would you experiment on a host-side product, such as a new listing-quality nudge, when the outcome you care about (bookings) is driven by guests who are not in your experiment? [Likely]
  14. Network effects among guests are weak on Airbnb, but interference through inventory is strong. Contrast this with social networks, and explain how the remedies differ. [Likely]
  15. Airbnb Experiences has far smaller inventory than Homes, with fixed time slots that sell out. How would interference and power differ from a Homes ranking test, and how would you design an Experiences ranking experiment? [Likely; 38]
  16. How would you use a market-level holdout ("launch everywhere except these N cities") to validate the global effect of a ranking system after launch? [Likely]
    • How do you choose holdout markets, and how do you analyze them (difference-in-differences, synthetic control)?
  17. When randomization at the right level is impossible, how would you estimate the causal effect of a marketplace-wide change? [Likely; 14]
    • How does a counterfactual model (predict what would have happened without the change) work, and how do you validate it?
    • What assumptions does synthetic control rely on, and how would you check them?

D. Variance reduction: CUPED, stratification and regression adjustment

  1. Explain CUPED. What is it, why does it reduce variance, and why does it not bias the treatment effect estimate? [Likely; 15]
    • How do you choose the covariate, and what is the optimal theta?
    • Why must the covariate be measured before the experiment starts?
  2. Booking is rare, and many users have no pre-period activity (new or logged-out users). How does CUPED perform for them, and what would you do instead? [Likely]
    • Could you use a model-predicted covariate, for example a predicted booking probability (CUPAC-style)? What are the risks?
  3. DoorDash describes CUPAC, which uses an ML model's prediction as the CUPED covariate. What must be true of that model (training window, features, version freezing) for the adjusted estimator to stay unbiased? What happens if the model was trained on data that overlaps the experiment period? [Likely; 28]
  4. How do CUPED and regression adjustment with several covariates relate? When would you use one over the other? [Likely]
  5. Explain stratified sampling versus post-stratification for variance reduction. Which strata would you use for Airbnb (market, platform, new versus returning guests)? [Likely]
  6. You applied CUPED and the variance fell by only 3%. Why might that happen for a booking metric, and what else would you try? [Likely]
  7. How does variance reduction change your sample-size calculation and your experiment duration? [Likely]
  8. Can you combine CUPED with ratio metrics and with the delta method? What do you need to be careful about? [Likely]
  9. You cap gross booking value at the 99th percentile and also apply CUPED. Should you cap before or after the adjustment? What does each order do to bias, variance and interpretation? [Likely]
  10. When would you choose a more sensitive design over variance reduction in the analysis? Examples include interleaving, paired designs and within-user designs. [Likely; 8]

E. Interleaving for search ranking

  1. What is interleaving, and why is it much more sensitive than an A/B test for comparing rankers? [Likely; 8]
    • Compare team-draft interleaving with balanced interleaving.
    • How do you assign credit for a booking, as opposed to a click, in an interleaved result list?
  2. Airbnb has described using interleaving to speed up search-ranking experiments. How would you set it up so that the interleaving verdict agrees with A/B results? [Likely; 8]
    • How would you measure agreement between interleaving and A/B tests over a set of historical launches?
    • What would you do if interleaving says ranker B wins but the A/B test is flat?
  3. Airbnb search is not a single click-and-done session. Guests search many times over days, and listings appear on map views and across pages. What complications does this create for interleaving? [Likely; 8]
    • How would you handle the same listing appearing in both rankers' lists, and duplicates across searches?
    • How do you attribute a booking that happens days later?
  4. Interleaving is a within-user design. How would you compute its significance (for example, a per-user preference sign test or a bootstrap over users), and why is per-impression analysis wrong? [Likely; 8]
  5. What can interleaving not tell you? Give examples of ranking changes that must still be A/B tested (for example, changes to price display, diversity or result-set size). [Likely; 8]
  6. Can you run interleaving and A/B tests on the same search traffic at the same time? How would you allocate traffic so that neither contaminates the other? [Likely; 8]
  7. Design a two-stage experimentation funnel: offline evaluation, then interleaving, then A/B test. What are the gates between stages? [Likely; 2][8]
    • Offline NDCG improves but interleaving is flat. What is your hypothesis?

F. Offline-online gap and ML-specific experimentation

  1. Your new ranking model improves offline NDCG by 2%, but the A/B test shows no booking lift. What are the possible reasons, and how do you debug? [Likely; 2][5]
    • Could position bias or feedback loops in the training data explain it?
    • How would you tell a modeling problem apart from an experiment-sensitivity problem?
  2. Airbnb's deep-learning search papers describe many launches that improved offline metrics but failed online. How would you build an offline metric that better predicts online wins? [Likely; 2][5]
  3. When training data come from the control model's own rankings, the treatment model is evaluated on data biased toward control. How does this affect A/B results, and how would you mitigate it? [Likely]
    • What role do exploration traffic or randomized-result buckets play?
  4. How would you use off-policy evaluation (counterfactual estimators such as inverse propensity scoring) to pre-screen ranking models before an online test? What does it require from logging? [Likely]
  5. Treatment and control models share a training pipeline. After launch, the new model's data feeds back into retraining. How would you measure that feedback-loop effect? [Likely]
  6. Your ranking model is retrained weekly with the same architecture on fresh data. Do you A/B test every refresh? Design a policy for automated model refreshes with lighter-weight validation and a rollback trigger. [Likely; 2][5]
  7. How would you A/B test a model whose outputs are cached or precomputed, such as listing embeddings or nightly batch scores? [Likely; 16]
    • What contamination can occur if the treatment's embeddings are also used by control's similar-listings carousel?
  8. How would you run an experiment on an ML model that serves several surfaces (search, similar listings, email recommendations) at once? [Likely]
  9. A new home-page browsing surface (such as Airbnb Categories) changes which searches guests run next. How would you separate its effect on search metrics from changes in ranking quality, and how would you test a ranking change that runs alongside it? [Likely; 39]
  10. How would you test a latency-increasing model (larger neural net) when latency itself hurts conversion? [Likely]
    • How would you separate the model-quality effect from the latency effect?
  11. How would you A/B test an LLM-powered customer-support feature, such as suggested replies for agents, where outcomes include resolution time, CSAT, escalations and policy errors? [Likely; 40]
    • Do you randomize by agent, by ticket or by guest, and what interference does each choice leave?
    • How do you combine offline LLM evaluation with the online test?
  12. How would you experiment on a fraud or trust model, where false positives block real guests and the true outcome (fraud, chargebacks) arrives weeks later? [Likely; 17]
    • Is holding out a random set of transactions from the fraud model ethical or practical? What would you propose?
  13. Treatment and control both generate logs that feed the next model's training data through the feature store. How do you tag and filter experiment traffic in feature and training pipelines so that one arm does not contaminate the next model? [Likely; 37]

G. Sequential testing, peeking and decision rules

  1. What goes wrong when a team peeks at an A/B test every day and stops as soon as p < 0.05? Quantify it roughly. [Likely; 1]
  2. Explain sequential testing approaches (group sequential with alpha spending, always-valid p-values or mSPRT). Which would you build into an experimentation platform, and why? [Likely; 18]
    • What do you give up in power compared with a fixed-horizon test?
    • How would you explain an "always-valid" confidence interval on a dashboard?
  3. Airbnb's 2014 post proposed a dynamic p-value threshold that is stricter early on. How does that compare with modern sequential methods? [Likely; 1]
  4. When is early stopping for harm appropriate, versus early stopping for benefit? [Likely]
  5. A test has run for its planned duration, and the p-value is 0.07. The PM wants two more weeks. What do you advise? [Likely]
  6. A bug in treatment is found and fixed on day 6 of a 21-day test. Do you restart, analyze only the post-fix period or analyze everything? What are the trade-offs? [Likely]
  7. How would you apply a Bayesian A/B testing approach to booking conversion? When does it help decision-making, and when does it mislead? [Likely]
    • How would you set priors from Airbnb's history of past experiments?
  8. When would you replace an A/B test with a multi-armed bandit, for example for email subject lines or the order of home-page categories? What do you lose for inference and for long-term measurement? [Likely; 34]
  9. Explain the winner's curse in experimentation. Airbnb has written about correcting for selection bias when summing the impact of launched winners. How would you correct it? [Likely; 19]
    • Leadership adds up the measured lifts of 40 launched experiments, and the total far exceeds the actual business growth. Why?
  10. How would you control the false discovery rate across hundreds of experiments and metrics on a platform? [Likely]
  11. Your platform runs about 1,000 experiments a year at alpha = 0.05 and 80% power, and only about 10% of ideas truly work. Roughly what fraction of 'significant' wins are false positives? How should that change launch criteria? [Likely; 35]

H. Novelty, primacy, learning and time effects

  1. How do you detect novelty or primacy effects in an Airbnb experiment, for example a redesigned listing page? [Likely]
    • How would you plot and test the treatment effect over time or by days since first exposure?
    • Why are novelty effects harder to see when most guests visit Airbnb only a few times a year?
  2. How do hosts learn and adapt to a new host-side feature, such as changing prices after a pricing tip? How would you design the experiment to capture the post-adaptation equilibrium? [Likely; 7]
  3. How would you handle seasonality and holidays, for example a test that spans the summer peak or the New Year period? [Likely]
    • Would you pause, extend or reweight?
  4. A city regulation forces a change (for example, a new fee display) on every listing in that market at once. How would you estimate its impact with a quasi-experiment, and what are the main threats (few units, parallel trends, spillover into nearby markets)? [Likely; 31][14]
  5. Experiments run during an external shock (for example a pandemic-scale demand drop or a major event in a city). How do you decide if the results still generalize? [Likely]
  6. Should Airbnb keep a universal holdout group that sees none of a year's launches? What would you measure, how large should it be, and what does it cost the business and the users in it? [Likely; 32]

I. Heterogeneous effects, segments and the launch decision

  1. The overall effect is flat, but new guests show +3% and returning guests show -2%. How do you decide whether to launch, and whether to personalize? [Likely]
    • How do you avoid Simpson's paradox in this reading?
    • How do you know the segment effect is not a multiple-testing artifact?
  2. How would you estimate heterogeneous treatment effects with ML (for example causal forests or uplift models), and how would you validate them? [Likely]
  3. You estimated heterogeneous treatment effects and want to show the feature only to segments that benefit. How do you turn the estimates into a targeting policy, and how do you validate the policy itself with a new experiment? [Likely; 33]
  4. A treatment helps in large markets and hurts in small markets. What could explain this in a marketplace, and what would you do? [Likely]
  5. The experiment improves bookings but reduces revenue per booking because guests choose cheaper stays. How do you make the launch call? [Likely; 6]
  6. Results are significant on iOS and web but not on Android. How do you reason about it? [Likely; 1]
  7. Walk me through the launch-review discussion you would lead when the primary metric is up, one guardrail is slightly down and the result is borderline significant. [Likely; 4] - What would you write in the experiment review document? - How do you communicate uncertainty to a VP who wants a single number?
  8. How would you size the actual business impact of a launch, as opposed to the experiment's measured lift? Consider trigger rate, ramp-up, interference and decay. [Likely; 19]

J. Experiment infrastructure and ML-platform design for experiments

  1. Design the assignment service for an experimentation platform that serves search, mobile apps and email, with consistent bucketing across surfaces. [Likely; 3] - How do layers or mutually exclusive groups work, and when do you need them? - How do you handle ramping from 1% to 50% without reshuffling users?
  2. Airbnb's mobile apps fetch experiment configuration at startup, and many users run older app versions or open the app offline. How do you handle version eligibility, cached assignments and late exposure logging in the analysis? [Likely; 36]
  3. Design the metrics computation pipeline behind a system like ERF: daily aggregates for thousands of metrics across hundreds of experiments. [Likely; 3][11] - How do you make it incremental, backfill-able and consistent with the company's metric definitions? - How do you make CUPED and the delta method efficient at that scale?
  4. Two notifications experiments (email and push) run at once, and both are subject to a global frequency cap. How does the cap create interference between the experiments and between arms, and how would you design around it? [Likely]
  5. How do you detect and handle interactions between concurrent experiments, for example two ranking experiments that both touch the same feature? [Likely]
  6. How would you design an experiment-quality monitoring system that automatically checks SRM, logging gaps, pre-period imbalance and metric anomalies? [Likely; 4]
  7. How would you architect shadow and canary deployment for a new ranking model before it enters an A/B test? [Likely] - What would you compare in shadow mode, and what kinds of failure would make you abort?
  8. How would you store and expose historical experiment results so that teams can run meta-analyses, such as proxy validation or calibrating priors? [Likely]
  9. How would you build self-serve power analysis into the platform, given that metric variances differ by market and season? [Likely]

K. Pricing, Smart Pricing and host-side experiments

  1. Design an experiment for a change to a host price-suggestion model. What is randomized, and what is measured? [Likely; 7][12] - Hosts may ignore suggestions. How does adoption affect your estimand (intent-to-treat versus compliers)? - How do you prevent treatment hosts' price changes from affecting control hosts' bookings?
  2. How would you estimate the price elasticity of demand from experiments without running illegal or unfair price discrimination on guests? [Likely] - Why do companies like Airbnb generally avoid randomizing the price shown to guests, and what do they do instead?
  3. Design an experiment for a weekly or monthly-stay discount nudge shown to hosts, where guests book long stays months ahead and the stay itself spans weeks. When can you read results, and what interim metrics would you trust? [Likely; 7]
  4. A pricing experiment shows host revenue up 4% in treatment, but marketplace-wide bookings are flat. How do you interpret this? [Likely; 12]
  5. How would you test a change in guest fees or the display of total price (for example, showing all-inclusive prices)? Which interference and regulatory concerns apply? [Likely]

L. Fairness, ethics and trust in experimentation

  1. How would you check that an experiment does not harm a protected group of guests or hosts, given that you may not observe protected attributes directly? [Likely; 20] - How might perceived race or gender be measured under privacy constraints (for example, in Airbnb's Project Lighthouse work), and how would that feed into experiment analysis?
  2. How would you add a fairness guardrail, such as a gap in booking acceptance rates between groups, to every experiment's scorecard? What power do you realistically have to detect a small change in that gap? [Likely; 20]
  3. Which experiments should not be run at all, or need special review, on a travel/housing marketplace? [Likely]
  4. How would you experiment on a trust and safety intervention, such as party prevention or booking restrictions, where the main outcome is a rare, severe harm? [Likely; 17]

M. Past-project deep dive: experimentation probes

  1. Tell me about an experiment you ran where the result surprised you. What did you do next? [Likely]
  2. Tell me about a time an A/B result was wrong or misleading, for example because of a bug, SRM, interference or novelty. How did you find out? [Likely]
  3. Describe a time you pushed back on launching a feature that had a "statistically significant" win. [Likely]
  4. Describe a time you changed how your team or org runs experiments, such as a new metric, a new method or new tooling. What was the impact? [Likely]
  5. What was the smallest effect you ever detected reliably, and what did you do to get enough sensitivity? [Likely]
  6. How did you decide between running an experiment and shipping on offline evidence? Give a time you chose not to A/B test. [Likely]
  7. Tell me about a time a limitation of your experimentation platform forced a workaround. What did you build, and what would you change if you owned the platform? [Likely]
  8. How do you work with data scientists, PMs and engineers when you disagree about how to read an experiment? [Likely]

N. Quick-fire concept checks (likely as follow-ups)

  1. What is the stable unit treatment value assumption (SUTVA), and where does it fail at Airbnb? [Likely; 13]
  2. Why is the t-test usually fine for non-normal metrics at Airbnb scale, and when does it break? [Likely]
  3. What is the difference between relative and absolute lift, and how do you build a confidence interval for relative lift? [Likely]
  4. What is the difference between a cluster-randomized design's unit of randomization and its unit of analysis, and how do you compute the standard errors? [Likely]
  5. What is Twyman's law, and how does it apply to experiment review? [Likely]
