05. ML Fundamentals and Modeling Depth: Ranking, GBDT vs. NN, Embeddings, Calibration, Features, Offline/Online Gap

Research note (read first). Adversarially reviewed 2026-10-14. The shared web-search budget was used up, so no new search queries could run. The proxy could not resolve arxiv.org, dl.acm.org, medium.com, prachub.com, teamblind.com or trueinterview.io. GitHub (pages and raw files) was the only reachable host. Fetched and read on review: the TrueInterview-derived Airbnb MLE guide (3 candidate reports, mainly 2026 Q1-Q2) [1]; the dated Airbnb question index [2]; the Airbnb company interview-process page [3]; and about 100 posts in a GitHub mirror of 1point3acres Airbnb interview posts. Only two of those posts contain ML rounds [4][5]. The other posts were checked and contain no ML content.

How references were checked. arXiv, ACM and Medium could not be opened directly. Airbnb paper and blog URLs marked "(URL confirmed)" were checked against the curated applied-ML index [30], which lists the same titles and URLs. URLs marked "(not verified)" are cited from prior knowledge or from Chapter 09. Check those before relying on them. The PracHub report [29] could not be opened. Its topics are taken from Chapter 09's summary and are tagged [Likely], not [Reported].

Tags. [Reported] means a fetched source names the prompt, the follow-up or the topic for an Airbnb MLE interview. [Likely] means an inference from the Senior MLE role, the reported round structure and Airbnb's published systems. A citation next to a [Likely] tag points to the paper or post behind the topic. It does not mean anyone was asked the question.

Format notes

  • There is no reported standalone "ML theory" round. Reported MLE loops have a phone screen, one coding round, one or two ML system design rounds (sometimes an extra one), an experience deep dive, core values and a hiring-manager conversation [1][3]. Fundamentals come up as depth probes inside the design rounds and the deep dive. The design rounds are "conversations without code", and "the first minutes of scoping are graded" [1].
  • The design rounds decide the loop. Rejections in the reports were attributed to design depth, not to coding. A borderline signal led to an extra design round instead of a pass [1]. One ML rejection was blamed on "jumping straight to model ideas"; the bar is "problem and label before model" [3].
  • Expected skeleton: decision supported → label → data → features → model → offline and online metrics → serving → experiment [1]. Interviewers assess problem framing, label definition and time window, concrete marketplace features (reviews, host responsiveness, booking completion, photos, location encodings), the link from offline to online metrics, and production behaviour (candidate generation, latency, freshness, cold start for new listings, monitoring) [1].
  • Interviewers steer hard. Reported examples: a lifetime-value round defined the label and then moved from regression to bucketized classification; a relisting round went into geohashing; search-ranking rounds pushed on latency, freshness and experimentation [1]. Treat a redirection as a hint and restate the narrower scope.
  • Reported prompts these probes hang off: search ranking end to end, ranking without a text query, personalized search ranking, user-embedding semantic search, listing LTV, relisting detection, cover-photo selection, high-quality listings, recommendations, and GenAI support retrieval and ranking [1][2]. 1point3acres loops add customer LTV and host pricing [4], plus listing recommendation and price prediction [5].
  • Senior (G9) bar: design carries more weight at Senior and above. Scope is scored separately from modeling difficulty, and a deep project with narrow reach "reads weaker" than a simpler one with wide impact [1]. Expect a "why" on every choice: label, GBDT vs. NN, metric, and what should move online. Practise each answer in 2-4 minutes, tied to an Airbnb surface.
  • Role context: Airbnb MLE postings stress training-serving skew, A/B testing, and feature and model selection. Shared infrastructure includes Chronon, Airbnb's open-source feature platform for consistent online and offline features [1][25].
  • Public Airbnb context interviewers know well: search moved from GBDT to deep NNs [6][7]; in-session listing embeddings [8]; Experiences ranking [9]; diversity in ranking [10][21]; multi-task journey ranking [11]; a customized pricing regression loss [12]; interleaving [13]; embedding-based retrieval [14].

Questions

A. Problem framing, labels and targets

  1. You are asked to estimate the lifetime value of a listing (property). Would you frame it as regression or as bucketized classification, and why? [Reported] [1][2]
    • The interviewer pushes you toward bucketized classification. What do you gain and lose? [Reported] [1]
    • How do you choose bucket boundaries, and how do you turn bucket probabilities back into an expected value? Would you use an ordinal loss or a plain softmax?
    • How do you handle the heavy right tail and listings that churn off the platform (censoring)?
  2. Before modeling LTV, agree on what "lifetime value" means here. Over what time window is the label defined, and how does that choice trade label delay against usefulness? [Reported] [1]
    • Which business decision does the LTV model support (host acquisition spend, host coaching, ranking), and how does that change the target and the metric? [Reported] [1]
  3. Design a guest (customer) lifetime-value model. How do the unit of prediction, the label and the features differ from listing LTV? [Reported] [4]
    • How would you validate an LTV model whose label only matures a year after prediction? [Likely] [18]
  4. How would you identify "high-quality listings"? What is your label, given that no ground truth exists? [Reported] [1]
    • Which proxies (reviews, rebook rate, cancellations, CS tickets, host responsiveness) would you combine? How do you avoid circularity with the ranking model's own exposure?
    • How would you use human raters to validate the proxy label, and how many ratings do you need? [Likely] [17]
  5. Choose a listing's cover photo to lift bookings. Is this a supervised problem, a ranking problem or a bandit problem? [Reported] [1]
    • What label would you train on, given that the cover photo you showed also caused the clicks?
    • How do you handle cold start for a new listing with no engagement on any photo? [Reported] [1]
    • When would you pick a contextual bandit over a supervised model plus an A/B test? [Likely]
  6. Detect relisting (a removed or banned listing reappears, possibly under a new account). What features and model family would you use? [Reported] [1][2]
    • Is this classification, similarity search or entity resolution? Defend the framing.
    • How does geohashing help you generate candidate pairs, and how does geohash precision trade recall against pair volume? [Reported] [1]
    • How would the model's outputs show up in online metrics, given that confirmed relistings arrive late and only for flagged cases? [Reported] [1]
    • How do you choose an operating threshold when false positives hurt legitimate hosts?
  7. For search ranking, what is the training label: click, long click, wishlist, booking request, accepted booking or completed stay without cancellation? [Likely] [6][11]
    • How do you weight these in a single objective vs. a multi-task model?
    • How do you deal with label delay (booking days after search)?
  8. Rank home search results when the user gives no text query (only location and dates, or nothing). What does "relevance" mean here, and what is the label? [Reported] [2]
    • With no query, popular listings dominate the logs. How do you avoid a popularity feedback loop and still give new listings exposure?
  9. A home page combines search, calendar availability and ranking. How do availability and date constraints enter the model: as hard filters, as features, or through the label (unavailable listings as negatives)? [Reported] [2]
  10. How would you define a negative example in Airbnb search: an impressed and not clicked listing, a clicked and not booked listing, or a random listing? What bias does each choice introduce? [Likely] [6][8]
  11. A booking requires host acceptance. How does that change your label definition and your model? [Likely] [6]
  12. You have far fewer bookings than clicks. How does label sparsity drive your choice of objective, model capacity and evaluation? [Likely] [6]

B. Learning to rank

  1. Compare pointwise, pairwise and listwise learning-to-rank objectives. Which would you start with for Airbnb search, and why? [Likely] [6]
    • Airbnb's search team reported success with a pairwise formulation. Why might pairwise suit a booking-or-not label inside a search?
  2. How do you build training pairs or lists from search logs (the booked listing vs. the other listings impressed in the same search)? [Likely] [6]
    • How many negatives per positive, and does sampling change the metric you are optimizing?
  3. Explain how LambdaRank / LambdaMART connects a pairwise loss to NDCG. Why can't you optimize NDCG directly with gradient descent? [Likely]
  4. Which offline metric would you use for search ranking (NDCG on the booked listing, MRR, recall@k, AUC), and why? Why might AUC improve while NDCG is flat? [Likely] [6]
  5. How do you handle position bias in logged search data? [Likely] [7]
    • Compare position as a feature (with dropout, or set to a constant at inference), inverse propensity weighting and randomization-based estimates.
    • How would you estimate position propensities at Airbnb without hurting users?
  6. Your ranker learns that cheap listings get booked. Why does pointwise use of price fail, and how would you encode price relative to the user's query and market? [Likely] [7]
  7. A ranker trained on bookings learns the majority guest's preference and pushes one listing segment to the top for everyone. Why does that happen, and how do you correct it? [Likely] [21]
  8. How do you rank diversely (avoid ten near-identical listings) without destroying relevance? [Likely] [10][21]
    • Is diversity a re-ranking step, a loss term or a list-aware model? What are the trade-offs?
    • How would you measure whether diversity helped bookings?
  9. How would you design a two-stage system (retrieval + ranking) for Airbnb search, and what does each stage optimize? [Reported] [1]
    • How do you stop the ranker from never seeing listings that retrieval filters out (selection bias)? [Likely] [14]
  10. Airbnb search is a multi-step journey (search → click → listing page → request → booking). How would you model it with multi-task learning? [Likely] [11]
    • How do you combine the task heads into a single ranking score?
    • How do you handle tasks with very different base rates and loss scales?
  11. You have several ranking objectives (bookings, price competitiveness, host quality). Compare a weighted multi-objective loss with distilling the blended objective into one model. [Likely] [27]
  12. How would you incorporate host-side objectives (acceptance likelihood, host preferences, fair exposure) into the ranking score? [Likely] [6]
  13. Personalized search ranking: which user signals would you use, and how do you avoid overfitting to a user's last click? [Reported] [2]
  14. How would you rank Airbnb Experiences differently from Stays, given much less data per item? [Likely] [9]
  15. Listings are also shown as pins on a map, where there is no clear top-to-bottom order. How do your ranking metric and position-bias assumptions change? [Likely] [26]

C. GBDT vs. neural networks

  1. When would you choose GBDT over a deep NN for a tabular ranking problem, and vice versa? [Likely] [6]
    • Airbnb moved search from GBDT to a NN and initially saw little gain. Why does that happen, and what made the NN eventually win?
  2. Compare a factorization machine with an embedding + DNN ranker for an Airbnb ranking problem. Which would you ship, and why? [Likely] [29]
  3. A NN on the same features as your GBDT performs worse. What do you check first: normalization, distribution shape, learning rate, capacity or label noise? How do you tell which one it is? [Likely] [6]
  4. GBDTs are invariant to monotone feature transforms; NNs are not. What normalization would you apply to price, review count and distance before a NN, and why might a log or Gaussian-rank transform matter for the NN but not the trees? [Likely] [6]
  5. How would you use a GBDT and a NN together (GBDT leaves as NN features, stacking or distillation)? [Likely]
  6. Why might listing-ID embeddings overfit in Airbnb search, when the same idea works for e-commerce item IDs? What about the booking-per-listing distribution makes it hard? [Likely] [6]
  7. Deep models are harder to interpret. How do you explain to a PM or a host why a listing ranked lower? [Likely] [6]
    • Compare SHAP, partial dependence plots and ablation-based importance. Which are trustworthy for a NN?
  8. Your NN is more accurate offline but has 3x the inference latency, and the interviewer pushes on the latency budget. How do you decide, and what closes the gap (distillation, caching, fewer candidates, smaller model)? [Reported] [1]
  9. How would you trade off precision, recall, latency and infrastructure cost when choosing a model for production ranking? [Likely] [29]
  10. How would you enforce a monotonicity requirement (for example, all else equal, a higher price must not raise the score)? [Likely] [7]
  11. You need to classify listings using text, photos and tabular attributes together. How would you design a multimodal model, and when is it worth it over a GBDT on hand-built features? [Likely] [22]

D. Embeddings and representation learning

  1. How would you learn listing embeddings from user click sessions? [Likely] [8]
    • Why treat a session as a "sentence" and use skip-gram with negative sampling?
    • What is the effect of using the booked listing as global context?
    • Why sample negatives from the same market, and what goes wrong if you sample uniformly?
  2. How do you produce an embedding for a brand-new listing with no sessions (cold start)? What are the failure modes of averaging nearby similar listings? [Likely] [8]
  3. Bookings are too sparse to learn per-user embeddings. How would you learn user-type and listing-type embeddings in the same space? [Likely] [8]
  4. How do you evaluate listing embeddings offline before they reach production? Which intrinsic checks (geography, price, style) and which extrinsic checks would you run? [Likely] [8]
  5. Design listing recommendations (similar listings, or listings for a guest). How would you evaluate an embedding-based recommender offline and online? [Reported] [1][5]
  6. Use embeddings for real-time personalization: how do you turn a user's in-session clicks and skips into ranking features, and how do you weight recent vs. older signals? [Likely] [8]
  7. Design user embeddings for semantic search (free-text queries such as "cabin with a hot tub near a lake"). [Reported] [2]
    • How would you align queries and listings in one embedding space?
    • Which training pairs and hard negatives would you use?
  8. How would you build embedding-based retrieval for Airbnb search and combine it with hard filters (dates, price, map bounds, guests)? Pre-filter vs. post-filter with ANN: what are the recall and latency trade-offs? [Likely] [14]
  9. Two-tower vs. cross-encoder: where in the stack would you use each, and why? [Likely] [14]
  10. Prices, photos and availability change daily. How do you keep embeddings and features fresh, and how do you measure what staleness costs you? [Reported] [1]
    • What breaks downstream when you retrain the embedding model and the space rotates? [Likely]
  11. Spatial features: compare geohash buckets, learned location embeddings and raw latitude/longitude. Why can raw lat/long be a poor NN input, and how does geohash granularity affect generalization? [Reported] [1][3]
  12. How would you use image embeddings from listing photos (room type, amenity detection, photo quality) as ranking or classification features? [Likely] [15][16]
  13. When does a pre-trained LLM or foundation-model embedding beat a domain-trained listing embedding, and when does it not? [Likely]

E. Calibration and probability quality

  1. What is calibration, and why does it matter if you only use the score to rank? Name Airbnb consumers that need a calibrated probability (combining multi-task heads, expected-value LTV, trust thresholds). [Likely]
  2. How do you measure calibration (reliability diagram, ECE, log loss vs. Brier score), and how do Platt scaling, isotonic regression and temperature scaling each fail? [Likely]
  3. You down-sample negatives 10:1 for training. How do you correct the predicted probabilities? [Likely] [6]
  4. Your model is well calibrated overall but miscalibrated for new listings and small markets. How do you detect and fix that? [Likely]
  5. You combine pClick x pBook|click x pAccept into one ranking score. What happens if one head is miscalibrated? [Likely] [11]
  6. Calibration drifts after a ranking change, because the model now trains on its own exposure. How do you monitor and recalibrate? [Likely]
  7. In the bucketized LTV model, how do you check that the bucket probabilities are calibrated enough to compute an expected value? [Likely] [1]

F. Feature engineering

  1. Name the most important feature families for Airbnb search ranking and justify each. Which are query-dependent (dates, guest count, location) and which are listing-only? [Likely] [6]
  2. Which concrete marketplace signals (reviews, host responsiveness, booking completion, photos) would you turn into features for listing quality or LTV, and how? [Reported] [1]
  3. How would you build a feature for "price relative to the market for these dates and this listing type"? [Likely] [7][12]
  4. How do you create features from reviews (count, average, recency, text) and historical CTR or booking rate without leaking future information? How do you guarantee point-in-time correctness in the training pipeline? [Likely] [25]
  5. How would you compute per-guest cumulative booking counts and first and last booking times as point-in-time-correct model features? [Likely] [2]
  6. How do you handle new listings whose historical features are missing: imputation, a missingness indicator or a separate model? How would you estimate their engagement? [Likely] [7]
  7. How do you handle high-cardinality categorical features (city, host ID, amenity sets) in a GBDT vs. a NN? [Likely]
  8. Which features would you compute in real time (session clicks, current filters), and which in batch? What does each choice cost? [Likely] [8]
  9. How do you detect and prevent training-serving skew (a feature computed differently online than offline)? What does a shared feature platform such as Chronon solve, and what does it not? [Likely] [1][25]
  10. How do you decide whether a new feature is worth its engineering and latency cost? What ablation and online test would you run? [Likely]
  11. Seasonality and lead time strongly affect demand. How would you encode check-in date, lead time and length of stay? [Likely] [12]
  12. How do you handle features that the ranking model itself influences (for example, a listing's CTR depends on how highly it was ranked)? [Likely] [7]

G. Offline evaluation and the offline/online gap

  1. How do you translate an offline metric (NDCG, AUC) into an expected online metric (bookings, revenue), and which A/B test would confirm it? [Reported] [1]
    • Have you validated the correlation between your offline metric and online lift across past launches?
  2. Offline NDCG improved, but the online A/B test is flat or negative. List the hypotheses you would investigate, in order: training-serving skew, logging bias, metric mismatch, novelty, low power, interference between listings. How do you rule each out? [Likely] [6][29]
  3. Why can a model trained on logs from the old ranker look good offline but fail online? How would off-policy evaluation (IPS, doubly robust) help, and what assumptions does it need? [Likely] [7][28]
  4. When would you use interleaving instead of an A/B test to compare two rankers? [Likely] [13]
    • What does interleaving measure well, and what can it not tell you (revenue, long-term effects)?
    • How do you validate that interleaving outcomes agree with A/B outcomes?
  5. How would you combine counterfactual (logged-data) evaluation with interleaving to filter ranking candidates before a full A/B test? [Likely] [28]
  6. How do you build an offline evaluation set that represents the online traffic mix (markets, devices, new vs. returning users)? [Likely]
  7. Your model wins in aggregate but loses in some markets or segments. How do you decide whether to launch? [Likely]
  8. How do you guard against overfitting to the offline evaluation set after many iterations? [Likely]
  9. Which primary metric should a ranking launch be judged on, and which guardrail metrics would block it? How do you make both robust to noise and gaming? [Reported] [2]
  10. How would you set up a long-term holdout for ranking changes, and what does it show that a two-week A/B test cannot? [Likely]
  11. What metric would you use when the goal is "a great stay", not just a booking (cancellations, reviews, rebookings)? [Likely] [11]
  12. Airbnb converts short-term actions into a predicted future value so that teams can compare trade-offs. How would you build such a model, and how would you check it before using it as an experiment metric? [Likely] [19]
  13. Once a model ships, which signals would you monitor to catch degradation before bookings drop? [Reported] [1]

H. Training, generalization and debugging

  1. How would you detect and fix overfitting in a deep ranking model trained on one year of search logs? [Likely] [6]
  2. How do you split data for time-dependent problems (random vs. temporal vs. by user/listing), and why does it matter at Airbnb? [Likely]
  3. How do you handle class imbalance (bookings much rarer than impressions): re-weighting, sampling or focal loss? Which of these break calibration? [Likely]
  4. How often would you retrain the ranker, and how do you decide (fixed schedule vs. drift-triggered)? [Likely]
  5. Model quality dropped after a holiday season. How do you tell concept drift from a data-pipeline bug? [Likely]
  6. How do you make training reproducible enough that a 0.5% offline gain is real and not noise? How many seeds or bootstrap resamples would you run? [Likely]
  7. How do you choose the loss for a pricing or demand model where under- and over-prediction have asymmetric costs? [Likely] [12]

I. Regression, forecasting and pricing-flavoured modeling

  1. Design a model that helps hosts set a price for each future date, using market demand. What exactly is the target: the booked price, the listed price, or a demand curve? [Reported] [4][5]
  2. Predict the nightly price for a brand-new listing with no booking history. What features and model would you use, and how do you evaluate it? [Reported] [5]
  3. Airbnb proposed a customized regression loss for price suggestions because true "optimal price" labels do not exist. How would you learn a price when you only see booked or not booked at a set price? [Likely] [12]
  4. How would you estimate demand elasticity when price is confounded with listing quality and seasonality? [Likely] [12]
  5. How would you evaluate a price-suggestion model offline when hosts can ignore the suggestion? [Likely] [12]
  6. How would you forecast occupancy or demand for a listing 90 days out, and which uncertainty estimates would you provide? When would you use quantile regression instead of mean regression for host-facing estimates? [Likely]
  7. Airbnb built an ML pipeline to predict the value of homes. How would you choose among candidate models for such a value model, and how would you check that it is fair across markets? [Likely] [18]

J. Classification, trust and quality models

  1. Design the label and model for detecting fake or fraudulent listings, when positives are rare and arrive late. How do you handle labels that only exist for cases an earlier model or rule flagged (selection bias)? [Likely]
  2. Predict party risk at reservation time. The label (a reported party) is rare and noisy. How would you build it, and which features would you refuse to use for fairness reasons? [Likely] [1]
  3. A property-damage risk score feeds a protection or screening decision. How do asymmetric error costs shape the threshold, and why does calibration matter here? [Likely] [1]
  4. Build a classifier that flags "family friendly" listings. Where do labels come from (weak supervision, rules, raters), and how do you measure precision on a long tail of listing types? [Likely] [29]
  5. How do you evaluate a classifier when precision matters far more than recall (for example, removing a host's listing)? [Likely]
  6. How would you use text (descriptions, messages) in a classifier: TF-IDF + GBDT, a fine-tuned transformer, or LLM embeddings? [Likely] [22]
  7. You must classify guest-host messages by intent, but no taxonomy exists yet. How would you discover the intent classes and then train a supervised classifier? [Likely] [23]
  8. How would you build an active-learning or human-in-the-loop process to label listing categories or quality? [Likely] [17]
  9. How would you check a ranking or trust model for unfair outcomes across host or guest groups? [Likely]

K. Retrieval and LLM modeling for support (recent senior loops)

  1. Recent senior loops asked ranking and retrieval questions about generative-AI support tools. How would you evaluate a retriever that finds the right policy passages for a support case (recall@k, nDCG on labeled pairs, LLM-as-judge)? [Reported] [1]
  2. Generating customer-support instructions from company policies: when would you fine-tune an LLM, and when would retrieval-augmented generation be enough? How would you measure hallucination and policy compliance? [Reported] [1][2]
  3. How would you rerank retrieved support documents, and what labels would you train the reranker on? [Likely] [1]

L. Experimentation-adjacent modeling (role focus)

  1. Your new ranker is evaluated by a user-randomized A/B test. Why can supply competition between listings bias the estimate, and how does that affect how you read a model's lift? [Likely] [13]
  2. How would you use a model-based surrogate metric (predicted booking value) to read experiments faster? What validation does the surrogate need? [Likely] [19]
  3. How would you use pre-experiment covariates (CUPED or regression adjustment) to reduce variance, and which model features make good covariates? [Likely]
  4. You cannot randomize a host-facing model (for example, a pricing tool hosts opt into). How would you estimate its causal effect, and how could an ML-based counterfactual help? [Reported] [2] - What are the failure modes of ML-predicted counterfactuals compared with a randomized control? [Likely] [20]
  5. Heterogeneous treatment effects: how would you model which users or markets benefit from a ranking change (uplift models, causal forests)? [Likely]
  6. If you can run only one experiment this quarter, how do you choose among three model improvements using offline evidence? [Likely]
  7. Which guardrail metrics would you set for launching a new ranking model, and how do you avoid guardrails blocking every launch through false alarms? [Likely] [24]

M. Senior-level depth probes (deep dive and design rounds)

  1. In the ML project you are walking us through, why did you choose this model, feature set or serving path over the alternatives? [Reported] [1]
  2. In your most impactful model, which single modeling decision mattered most, and how do you know? [Likely] [1]
  3. Tell me about a time a simpler model (heuristic, logistic regression, GBDT) beat a more complex one in production. Why did it win? [Likely]
  4. What did you choose not to model, and why? [Likely]
  5. How did you choose your offline metric, and did it ever mislead you? [Likely] [1]
  6. Given twice the data, twice the features or twice the model capacity, which would you take for Airbnb search, and why? [Likely]
  7. The interviewer redirects you mid-design (for example, from regression to classification). How do you restate the narrower scope and adapt your modeling plan? [Reported] [1]
  8. How do you explain a ranking model's trade-offs to a PM who only cares about bookings? [Likely]

06. General Backend System Design: Booking, Availability, Search, Messaging, Payments and Feature Store

Research note. This chapter was re-checked on 2026-10-14. Only GitHub-hosted sources could be reached; Glassdoor, Blind, LeetCode, Medium, Hello Interview, IGotAnOffer, PracHub, TrueInterview and the Airbnb Tech Blog did not resolve, and the web-search budget was used up. The chapter rests on aggregated 2025 to 2026 candidate reports on GitHub [1][2][3][4], a community prep guide [5], a crowd-sourced question list [6], the Tech Interview Handbook [7], Airbnb's open-source Chronon repository [8], and English and Chinese 1point3acres interview reports mirrored on GitHub [11]-[45]. A question is tagged [Reported] only when a cited source reports it, or a very close variant, for Airbnb. All other questions are [Likely]: inferences from Airbnb's products and the senior bar those sources describe. Most mirrored 1point3acres posts carry no date. Where a date is given, it is 2025 or 2026 except [21] (2023).

Format notes

  • Rounds and length. Mid-level loops usually have one design round and senior loops two, each about an hour of discussion with no code. Senior loops can also move system design into the phone stage. A design round is sometimes added after the onsite when the signal is borderline [1]. Several candidates describe that extra round, for example a classic booking prompt focused on preventing double booking [25] [1]. The Tech Interview Handbook lists one "System Design/architecture" round in the general onsite [7]. One 2026 report for the 1-to-3-year level (G8) describes a system design screen plus a second system design round at the onsite [5].
  • Interviewer and track. MLE candidates usually get one or two ML system design rounds instead (see chapter 03) [2][4]. Generic backend prompts still appear at senior level [1][3], and staff loops have included both a distributed job scheduler and Airbnb booking [29].
  • Senior bar. Senior loops "expect depth one candidate called staff level" [1]. Several rejected senior candidates were told their design round fell short of the senior bar [22][32][21].
  • Common failures. Running out of time while trying to cover everything (an in-memory SQL database prompt) [1]. "Chased perfection on the chat schema instead of aligning on trade-offs" [1]. Needing too many hints on API design [38]. Talking about scaling when the interviewer wanted to go deep on APIs and database design [16].
  • What gets probed. Schemas and indexes that make the stated queries cheap (top-N chats, time and location filters); double booking under contention, idempotent refunds and delivery guarantees [1]. Interviewers often treat the supporting services (pricing, search, notifications) as given and focus on the data schema and one or two core flows [19].
  • What this means for an MLE. Expect a classic marketplace prompt (booking, waitlist, chat, notifications, ticketing, job scheduler). Expect the interviewer to probe whatever your background makes natural: feature stores, logging pipelines and experiment assignment.

Questions

A. Booking and reservation system

  1. Design Airbnb's booking system: guests search for available listings and book them, and the system must never double-book a listing. [Reported] [1][3][5][25][27][29]
    • What functional requirements would you commit to in the first few minutes, and what would you explicitly leave out?
    • Is a booking request a strongly consistent write? Which parts of the flow can be eventually consistent?
    • How do you guarantee two guests cannot book overlapping dates on the same listing at the same moment?
    • Would you use pessimistic row locks, optimistic concurrency with version numbers, or a uniqueness constraint on (listing, night)? Why?
    • How do you paginate and index availability search results? [1]
    • Follow-up: a big local event creates a hot partition. Which keys get hot, and how do you shed or queue load without letting anyone book a listing that is already taken? [5]
  2. Design a hotel booking system, with rooms that have inventory counts rather than unique homes. [Reported] [3]
    • How does inventory-count booking differ from Airbnb's one-unit-per-listing model?
    • How do you handle overbooking policies, if any are allowed?
  3. Walk through the state machine of a reservation: request, pending host approval, accepted, paid, checked in, completed, cancelled or altered. [Likely]
    • Where do you persist state transitions, and how do you make them auditable?
    • How do you expire a booking request the host never answers within 24 hours?
  4. How would you implement a temporary hold on dates while the guest is on the checkout page? [Likely]
    • How long should the hold last, and what releases it?
    • What happens if the payment succeeds after the hold has expired?
  5. Design Instant Book and Request to Book. How do the two flows differ in consistency and latency requirements? [Likely]
  6. How do you handle reservation alterations, such as changing the dates or guest count on an existing booking? [Likely]
    • How do you avoid releasing the old dates before the new dates are secured?
  7. Design the cancellation flow, including refunds that depend on the cancellation policy and the time left before check-in. [Likely]
  8. Design a waitlist system for a hot listing, with APIs to join, check position and leave, and notifications when spots open under high contention. [Reported] [1][3][20]
    • How do you notify the next person fairly, with a deadline to claim?
    • How do you avoid a thundering herd when a slot opens?
  9. Design a listing booking system in which users can join a waitlist when a listing is booked, and the first 20 waitlisted users are notified when it becomes available. Pricing, search and notification services already exist. [Reported] [19]
    • What is your data schema for bookings and waitlist entries?
    • How do you guarantee that exactly the first 20 users are notified once, even if the release event is delivered twice?
  10. How would you shard the reservations database as Airbnb grows across regions? [Likely]
    • Would you shard by listing, by guest or by host? Which queries become cross-shard?
  11. How do you make the "Reserve" API idempotent when the mobile client retries on a flaky network? [Likely] [9]
  12. Booking needs data from several services: listing, pricing, availability, payments and trust. How do you coordinate the transaction across them? [Likely] [9]
    • Would you use a saga, two-phase commit or an orchestrator? What are the compensating actions?
  13. Design a flight booking system that supports connecting flights while prices change. [Reported] [13]
    • How do you search for itineraries with connections (graph search, precomputed routes)?
    • Under a partition, and in normal operation, do you favor availability or consistency for prices and for seats (PA versus PC)? Agree on this before you design.

B. Availability calendar

  1. Design the availability calendar service for millions of listings, in which hosts block dates and set minimum and maximum nights and per-night prices. [Likely] [1][3]
    • What is the data model: one row per listing-night, interval ranges or bitmaps?
    • What are the read-to-write ratios for search, listing-page views and host edits?
  2. Design Google Calendar (events, recurrence, invites, free/busy). [Reported] [3][39]
    • How do you store and expand recurring events efficiently?
    • What schema supports fast queries such as "is everyone free from 2 to 3pm" across many calendars?
  3. Hosts sync calendars with other platforms through iCal. How do you import and export them without causing double bookings? [Likely]
    • What polling frequency would you use, and how do you resolve conflicts between an external block and a pending Airbnb request?
  4. How do you answer "which listings in Paris are available from June 3 to June 7 for 4 guests" at search time? [Likely] [1]
    • Do you pre-compute availability into the search index, or filter after retrieval?
    • How stale can the search index's availability be? What happens if a user clicks a listing that has just become unavailable?
  5. Design the split-stay feature, which offers two listings that together cover a guest's dates when no single listing does. [Likely] [1][25]
    • How do you search pairs of listings without an O(n squared) blow-up?
    • What changes if you allow three-way splits?
  6. How do you handle time zones and check-in and check-out boundaries for listings around the world? [Likely]
  7. A host edits the calendar while a guest is booking the same dates. Which write wins, and how do you guarantee correctness? [Likely]
  8. How would you propagate availability changes to search, pricing and ML ranking features within seconds? [Likely]
    • Would you use change data capture (CDC) to a log, dual writes or an outbox pattern?
  9. Walk through the data flow after a user modifies their profile information: which services, caches and indexes must update, and in what order? [Reported] [12]

C. Search and discovery backend

  1. Design the Airbnb home page: search, availability and ranking. [Reported] [1][3]
    • What are the latency budgets for retrieval, filtering and ranking?
    • How does the page behave when the ranking service is slow or down?
  2. Design ranking for home search results when the user has typed no text query (only location, dates and guests). [Reported] [3]
  3. Design a query system over user activity that filters by time window and geography. [Reported] [1][3]
    • What index structures would you use for the geo part (geohash, S2 cells, quadtrees) and for the time part?
    • How do you support queries such as "views in this bounding box in the last 7 days" at scale?
  4. Design a map-based search: as the user pans and zooms, return the listings within the viewport. [Likely]
    • How do you avoid hammering the backend on every pan event?
    • How do you cluster pins when thousands of listings fall in the viewport?
  5. Design a full-text search system: index building, query processing and integration with the underlying storage. [Reported] [13]
    • How do you optimize query latency and the efficiency of index updates?
    • When would you do a full reindex rather than incremental updates for listings, prices and availability?
  6. Design typeahead (autocomplete) search that returns matching results in real time as the user types a prefix, for example for destinations. [Reported] [34]
    • What data structures and storage would you use, and how do you rank and cache prefix results?
  7. Design semantic search over user and listing embeddings. [Reported] [3]
    • How do you choose and operate an approximate nearest-neighbor index (HNSW, IVF-PQ) next to structured filters?
    • How do you re-embed and re-index without downtime?
  8. A host with more than 100 listings finds the host listings page very slow. Redesign the aggregation from the bottom up. [Reported] [1][3][25]
    • What would you pre-compute, cache or paginate?
    • How do you avoid N+1 calls across service boundaries?
  9. Design cursor-based pagination for search results that stays stable when ranking or availability changes between page loads. [Likely] [1][41]
  10. How would you cache search results, given that many users query the same location and dates but prices and availability keep changing? [Likely]
  11. Design the wishlist service, including shared wishlists among group members. [Likely]

D. Messaging and notifications

  1. Design a group chat system (WhatsApp- or Slack-like): create groups, add members, send messages and view history. [Reported] [3][4][5][36][37][45]
    • The interviewer focuses on the database tables. What schema makes the required queries cheap?
    • How do you guarantee per-conversation ordering and exactly-once display?
    • How do you support presence and read receipts? [1]
  2. Design group chat with an inbox that shows a user's top 20 chats, ordered by the most recent message or by activity over a configurable window. [Reported] [1][32][33][34][35]
    • How do you maintain this inbox in real time without scanning every group's messages?
    • How do you notify online and offline users of each new message (long polling, WebSockets, an asynchronous queue)? [35]
    • How do you route messages across many WebSocket servers, and how do you recover when one fails? [32]
  3. Design a group-only instant messaging system with groups of 10,000 to 100,000 members. [Reported] [17]
    • Fan-out on write or fan-out on read? How do you avoid write amplification, and how do you shard?
    • Which delivery semantics do you choose, and how do you de-duplicate with message IDs?
    • How do you enforce ordering within a group (partition by group ID, a sequence generator)?
  4. Design guest-host messaging on Airbnb: messages tied to a listing or reservation, with read receipts and translation. [Likely]
    • How do you store the conversation so it is linked to the reservation and visible to support agents?
    • Where would you hook in fraud or off-platform-payment detection without adding latency to sending?
  5. Design a unified notification platform for guests, hosts and employees, covering event-triggered sends (an email when a guest books, a door lock code by SMS on check-in morning) and batch campaigns (promotions to prospective hosts). [Reported] [1][3][4][26][40]
    • How do you prioritize transactional notifications over marketing?
    • How do you de-duplicate, rate-limit per user and respect quiet hours across time zones?
    • What happens when a downstream provider (APNs, FCM, an SMS gateway) is slow or failing?
  6. How would you design per-user notification preferences and channel fallbacks (push, then email, then SMS)? [Likely]
  7. Design a customer-support ticketing system that routes tickets to the right agent queue. [Reported] [1][3][26][27]
    • How do you implement agent claim rules by language and location, and measure first-response time? [1]
    • How do you combine rules-based routing with an ML classifier, and fall back when the model is down?
  8. Design the support ticket lifecycle: users file tickets, tickets move through created, pending assignment, assigned and resolved, and users and agents chat inside the ticket. [Reported] [25]
    • The prompt is broad. Which 70 percent would you design first if time ran short?
  9. Design a ticket system with a watchlist or dashboard of open tickets and search by status, priority and creation date. [Reported] [21]
  10. Design a system that generates customer-support instructions from company policies, for example with an LLM. [Reported] [3]
    • How do you keep generated instructions in sync when a policy changes?
    • How do you evaluate correctness before showing them to agents?
  11. Design a system that sends reminder messages (check-in instructions, review requests) at specific times relative to each reservation. [Likely] [26]

E. Payments, payouts and financial correctness

  1. Design a payment system for hundreds of millions of daily active users, covering authentication, transaction processing and concurrency. [Reported] [15]
    • What is an idempotency key? Who generates it, and how long do you keep it? [9]
    • How do you separate the local DB transaction from the remote payment-provider call?
  2. A payment service runs on two databases and can no longer handle the traffic. How do you add sharding? [Reported] [14]
    • How do you choose the shard key and migrate live data without double-charging or losing writes?
    • How do you handle cross-shard transfers and reporting queries after the split?
  3. Design a digital wallet service (Google Wallet-style): stored balances, top-ups, transfers and transaction history. [Reported] [16][18]
    • Expect the interviewer to go deep on the APIs and the database schema rather than on scaling. What tables and constraints keep balances correct?
  4. Design host payouts: scheduling, splits among co-hosts, multiple currencies and payout methods. [Likely] [1]
  5. Design refunds across multiple payment methods and dates, honoring a priority order. [Likely] [10]
  6. How do you make refunds idempotent when the refund request, the provider callback and an agent's manual action can all arrive more than once? [Likely] [1]
  7. Design a double-entry ledger for Airbnb's payments platform. [Likely]
    • How do you reconcile your ledger against the payment processor's settlement files?
  8. Design a banking-like service with deposit, withdraw, transfer and balance-history queries by time. How would you turn it into a distributed service that handles concurrent transactions? [Likely] [1][16]
  9. Design a currency-conversion and price-display service that shows consistent prices from search through checkout. [Likely]
    • How do you lock an exchange rate for a quote?
  10. Design a retryer or retry library for calls to unreliable downstream services. [Likely] [1][19]
    • How do you decide which errors are retryable?
    • How do you combine exponential backoff, jitter, deadlines and circuit breakers?
  11. How would you design fraud checks into the payment path without hurting checkout latency? [Likely]

F. Data, logging and analytics infrastructure

  1. Design a highly interactive data analysis system. [Reported] [6]
    • What latency problem appears if you start with batch Spark processing?
    • How would you use Flink with Kafka for streaming aggregation?
    • How does recovery work when a Flink job fails (checkpoints, offsets, exactly-once)?
  2. Design an ad-click (or listing-click) event aggregation system. [Reported] [3]
    • How do you handle late and duplicate events?
    • How do you reconcile real-time counts with batch-corrected counts?
  3. Design a user event aggregation system, including its API, database, monitoring and SLOs. [Reported] [11]
    • Which SLOs would you commit to (freshness, completeness, query latency), and how would you alert on each?
  4. Model cumulative booking metrics, including the first and last booking times per user or listing. [Reported] [3]
    • Would you compute this incrementally or recompute it? How do you backfill when the logic changes?
  5. Design the logging pipeline that captures search impressions, clicks and bookings for ranking training data. [Likely]
    • How do you join impressions to bookings that happen days later?
    • How do you guarantee the logged features match what the model saw at serving time?
  6. Design a metrics and dashboard system for marketplace health (bookings, nights, supply) with daily and real-time views. [Likely]
  7. How would you design data-quality checks and alerting for core tables that feed models and experiments? [Likely]
  8. An existing production system has a high error rate. Design changes to make it more reliable and faster. [Reported] [16]
    • How do you find where the errors come from before you redesign anything?

G. Feature store and ML platform infrastructure

  1. Design a feature store that serves offline training and low-latency online ranking from one feature definition. [Likely] [8]
    • How do you guarantee point-in-time correctness in training backfills?
    • How do you measure and alert on online/offline consistency?
  2. Design the pipeline that measures online/offline feature consistency by logging online feature fetches, replaying their keys and timestamps as a backfill, and comparing the results. [Likely] [8]
    • At what sampling rate would you log, and how do you attribute a mismatch to a specific feature or pipeline?
  3. How would you support windowed aggregations (for example a listing's bookings in the last 7 days) in both streaming and batch? [Likely] [8]
    • How do you handle very skewed keys, such as a popular listing or a bot user, in the aggregation?
  4. Design the online serving layer of a feature store for a ranking request that fetches features for 1,000 candidate listings in under 50 ms. [Likely] [8]
    • Which key-value store would you choose? How do you batch, cache and tolerate tail latency?
  5. A team adds a new feature to a ranking model already in production. Design the backfill, validation and rollout path. [Likely] [8]
  6. How do you version features, and what happens to running models when a feature definition changes? [Likely]
  7. Design a model-serving platform that supports canary releases, shadow traffic and rollback. [Likely]
  8. Design an embedding-serving service that keeps listing embeddings fresh as listings and behavior change. [Likely]
  9. Design the serving backend for host price suggestions: which parts are batch-computed, which are computed per request, and how do you keep them consistent with the calendar? [Likely] [42][43]
  10. Design the serving path for listing recommendations ("similar listings"): what is precomputed, what is retrieved online, and how is it kept fresh? [Likely] [43]
  11. Design a job scheduler for about ten million users. [Reported] [1][3][4][28][30][31]
    • How do you guarantee each job runs at least once, and when do you need exactly-once?
    • How do you scale the dispatcher and avoid one hot time bucket (for example midnight UTC)?
    • How would you shard the job database? How do queue features such as visibility timeouts and delayed delivery (for example in SQS) work underneath? [28]
  12. Design a distributed job scheduler with task dependencies, priorities, high availability, retries and monitoring, for example for ML training and backfill DAGs. [Reported] [28][29]
  13. A cache holds derived values that depend on other cache entries (a DAG). Design the invalidation and update path so that dependents are always correct when an input changes. [Likely] [44]

H. Experimentation platform (backend view)

  1. Design an A/B testing platform. [Reported] [3]
    • How do you assign users to variants deterministically and consistently across web, iOS and Android?
    • How do you handle logged-out users who later log in?
    • How do you log exposures so analysis is not diluted?
  2. Design the assignment service's API and latency budget, given that every page load may evaluate dozens of experiments. [Likely]
    • Do you evaluate on the server or in a client SDK? How do you push config changes?
  3. How would you support mutually exclusive experiment layers and holdouts? [Likely]
  4. Design the metrics pipeline that computes experiment scorecards daily for hundreds of experiments and thousands of metrics. [Likely]
  5. How would you design a kill switch or automated guardrail that stops an experiment when a key metric degrades? [Likely]
  6. Design a network-aware experiment for a "Wi-Fi badge" on listings, where treatment effects can spill over between listings. [Reported] [3]
    • How would the platform support cluster or market-level randomization?

I. Generic distributed-systems building blocks (asked at Airbnb)

  1. Design a distributed key-value store. [Reported] [1][23][24]
    • Walk through the CAP trade-offs, consistent hashing and vector clocks. [1]
    • How do you support get, put, delete and a paginated prefix scan (get(key*)) under eventual consistency and high availability? [23]
    • How do you handle node failure, rebalancing and fault recovery? [24]
  2. Design a highly reliable, highly available distributed key-value system that supports bulk upload, multiple versions per key and TTLs. [Reported] [22]
    • How does a bulk upload become visible atomically, and how do you garbage-collect expired versions?
  3. Design an in-memory SQL engine. Agree on scope first. [Reported] [1]
    • Which subset of SQL (select, where, join, group by) do you commit to, and why?
  4. Design an online two-player chess game. [Reported] [3][4][38]
    • What does the API look like? How do you sync game state in real time and handle disconnects and clocks?
  5. Design a rate limiter for Airbnb's public APIs and internal services. [Likely]
  6. A partner API complains that your service calls it too often. Redesign the integration to batch the calls without breaking freshness guarantees. [Likely] [19][41]
  7. Design a distributed cache in front of the listing service, and explain invalidation when a host edits a listing. [Likely]
  8. Design account merge and user de-duplication for people who create multiple accounts. [Likely] [1]
    • How do you merge reservations, reviews and payment methods safely?

J. Other Airbnb product surfaces

  1. Design the reviews system: double-blind reviews revealed only when both parties submit or the window closes. [Likely]
    • How do you implement the reveal-at-deadline logic reliably?
  2. Design the listing creation and photo upload pipeline, including image processing and moderation. [Likely]
  3. Design Experiences booking with time slots, group capacity and a waitlist. [Likely] [3]
  4. Design a multi-traveler itinerary service that combines stays and experiences, for example filling a layover with experiences. [Likely] [10]
  5. Design a relisting detection system that catches a banned host relisting the same property under a new account. [Reported] [2][3]
    • How do you use geohashing and photo or text similarity at scale?
  6. Design a listing lifetime-value estimation service and its batch and online parts. [Reported] [2][3][42]
  7. Design the trust and safety screening pipeline that scores every booking before confirmation. [Likely]
    • How do you keep it off the critical path where possible, and do you fail open or closed when it is down?

K. Cross-cutting senior probes (apply to any prompt)

  1. What are your back-of-the-envelope numbers for this design (QPS, storage, peak-to-average ratio)? [Likely] [1][17]
  2. You have 45 minutes. Which two components would you deep-dive, and why those? [Likely] [1]
  3. Write out the API and the core tables before you draw any boxes. Which queries does each index serve? [Likely] [1][16][19]
  4. How do you deploy a schema change to the reservations table with zero downtime? [Likely]
  5. How would you run this system across multiple regions? What happens during a region failover in the middle of a booking? [Likely] [17]
  6. What metrics, alerts and SLOs would you define for this service? [Likely] [11]
  7. Which part of this design would you A/B test, and what could go wrong with the test because of the architecture (caching, sticky assignment, logging gaps)? [Likely]
  8. How would you migrate from the existing monolith to this service-oriented design incrementally? [Likely]
  9. If traffic grew 10x tomorrow, what breaks first? [Likely]
  10. What would you simplify if you had to ship a first version in two weeks? [Likely] [1]
  11. How do you secure personal data (addresses, IDs, payment details) in this design, and who can access it? [Likely]

  45. Airbnb onsite report: "classic design group chat", approached like a WhatsApp design, FlowSight/coding (1p3a #3). https://github.com/FlowSight/coding/blob/main/company%20wise/airbnb/1p3a/3.md

07. Past Project / Technical Experience Deep Dive: Reported Prompts and Probing Follow-ups for Senior MLEs

Research note (read first). This chapter was re-verified on 2026-10-14. The shared web-search budget was used up, so no new search queries could run. The network proxy could not resolve arxiv.org, medium.com, airbnb.tech, careers.airbnb.com or trueinterview.io, and the Glassdoor, Blind, LeetCode, IGotAnOffer and InterviewQuery hosts were unreachable earlier. GitHub was reachable, so this chapter is built from pages fetched live there: a mirror of about 100 Airbnb interview posts from 1point3acres (the FlowSight mirror, in Chinese and English), the TrueInterview Airbnb guides (CC BY 4.0), which are based on 3 MLE reports and 41 SWE reports from 2025 Q4 to 2026 Q2, and the TrueInterview Airbnb question list (dated entries to September 2026). In this review, [1]-[4], [5], [6]-[10], [14], [15], [17], [18], [19], [21], [23], [24], [28] and [34]-[37] were re-fetched and checked against the questions that cite them. Three tags were corrected: the whiteboard prompt (now [Likely]), the API and database probe (now [Likely], because that report came from a system design round), and the time-zone and "code to project" prompts (still [Reported], but from the core values round). [30]-[33] could not be fetched (DNS failure) and are cited from prior knowledge. Tags: [Reported] means a cited source says a candidate was asked this, or something very close to it, at Airbnb. [Likely] means an inference from the role, the senior bar and Airbnb's products; a citation on a [Likely] question only supplies context. Most deep-dive reports come from SWE and infrastructure loops, but the MLE guide says the MLE track runs the same round format [1].

Format notes

Aspect What sources report
Name on the schedule "Experience deep dive", "Technical Experience Interview", "Project Deep Dive", "Resume deep dive", "Project presentation", or for ML roles "ML Experience" [1][2][6][7][9][16][17]
Length 45-60 min. One candidate's round was booked for 1 hour and ran about 90 minutes [1][8][9][14]
Interviewer Usually a senior IC or an engineering manager, and sometimes a senior manager from a neighbouring team, who walks through the project step by step [16][21][25][36]. A separate hiring-manager (HM) call often repeats the deep dive and adds behavioral questions [9][22][23]
Shape One project, taken apart: architecture, the decisions that were yours, trade-offs, measured impact. Interviewers keep asking "until they heard what they wanted to hear" [1][6][16]. In at least one loop the deep dive and a coding exercise shared a single session [26]. Behavioral and values questions are scored in separate rounds run by employees from any function [5]
Senior / L5 bar The perceived scope of your work is scored on its own. Lead with problem size, ownership and outcome, not model details [1]. The round "also drives leveling" [2]. Scope judgements here can overturn strong technical rounds [2][3]
Weight One recruiter told a candidate "the deep dive round is the most important", and Airbnb would not re-run it [18]. Several senior rejections were pinned on this round alone [8][18][20]
Reported failure modes Opening walkthrough too long: keep it near 10 minutes, and one candidate's 20+ minutes ate the follow-up time [2]. A narrow project reads as below level, and a polished success story with no real mistakes is marked down [3]. A project too complex to explain or abstract quickly [8][14]. Generic answers without technical detail [18]. "No technical challenge visible" [10]. Background judged a poor match for the team [20]
MLE-specific twist One MLE candidate's "ML Experience" round turned into an unexpected ML design question [7]. Prepare to whiteboard your project's system the way you would a design round
Prep advice from reports Know every line on your resume: one candidate was grilled on a project that took one line [12]. Bring metrics, a conflict and a rejected alternative [2][3]. At staff level, compare your system with how Airbnb solved a similar problem on its tech blog, and say why you chose your approach [24]

How to use this chapter: choose one flagship ML project that touched an experiment or online metric, with clear cross-team reach. Practise a 10-minute arc, then drill every question below against that project. Prepare a backup project for questions that ask for a different story, such as a failure or a conflict.

Questions

A. Opening, project selection and the 10-minute arc

  1. Walk me through the project you are proudest of. [Reported] [16][11][29]
    • Why this project and not another one on your resume?
    • Can you give me the two-minute version before we go deep? [Likely] [3]
  2. Pick one recent project and walk me through it from start to finish. [Reported] [19][25]
    • Where do you want to start: the problem, the system or the result?
  3. Tell me about the most interesting project you have worked on. [Reported] [22]
  4. Show me a project you are proud of that was cross-organizational or cross-functional. [Reported] [11]
    • Which teams outside yours had to change something for it to ship?
  5. Walk me through the projects on your resume. What were you responsible for on each? [Reported] [13]
    • You wrote one line about this older project. Tell me about it in detail. [Reported] [12]
  6. Walk through one ML project end to end, covering the decisions that were yours and the impact you measured. [Reported] [1]
    • Who used its output, and how many users, listings or dollars did it touch? [Likely] [1]
    • Which metric moved, and which teams did you have to align with? [Likely] [1]
  7. If you had to describe the project's scope in one sentence (problem size, your ownership, outcome), what would it be? [Likely] [1]
  8. Can you draw the overall system on the whiteboard while you explain? [Likely] [14]
  9. Walk me through your background, and why Airbnb specifically? Which project best shows why you fit this team? [Reported] [3]

B. Problem framing and business context

  1. What problem was the project solving, and why did it matter to the business at that time? [Reported] [17][6]
    • What would have happened if nobody had done this project?
  2. Why did the project start, and how did you get stakeholders to commit to it? [Reported] [2][3]
    • Which stakeholders outside your team had to commit, and what did they give up? [Reported] [3]
  3. Set the implementation aside: what was the user problem, and how did the product change for users? [Reported] [2]
    • How would you explain the impact to a product manager who never looked at the model? [Likely]
  4. What decision or product surface did the model's output feed into? [Likely] [1]
    • Who consumed the score: a ranker, a rule engine, an ops team or a user-facing UI?
  5. How did you turn a vague product goal into a measurable prediction problem? [Likely] [1]
    • What was the label, and why that label rather than a nearby proxy?
  6. What was the scale: traffic, QPS, data volume, number of entities? [Reported] [10]
    • What about that scale made the problem hard?
  7. Why did you split the work into a short-term plan and a long-term plan? Why was the long-term plan needed at all? [Reported] [10]
  8. Was ML actually necessary here? What was the heuristic or rules baseline? [Likely]
    • By how much did ML beat the baseline, and was the extra complexity worth it?
  9. How did you size the opportunity before you started? [Likely]
  10. How did you choose the training objective or loss, and how closely did it track the business metric you cared about? [Likely]
    • In a two-sided marketplace, whose outcome did the objective favour, guests or hosts, and how did you balance the two? [Likely]

C. Ownership: what was yours versus the team's

  1. What was your specific role on the project? [Reported] [15][13]
    • Which decisions did you personally make, and which did you inherit?
  2. How was the work divided across the team? [Reported] [9]
    • Who else was on the project, and what did each person own?
  3. Did you lead the project, or contribute to it? How many people did you direct or influence? [Likely] [1]
  4. Were the metrics you analysed already there, or did you add them? [Reported] [10]
  5. What did you do that nobody asked you to do? [Likely]
  6. If you had left halfway through, what would not have happened? [Likely]
  7. Whom did you influence outside your own group, and how? [Likely] [2]
  8. After launch, who owned the model in production (on-call, retraining, deprecation)? Did ownership move to another team? [Likely]

D. Data, labels and features

  1. Where did the training data come from, and what were its biggest quality problems? [Likely] [1]
    • How did you detect and handle label leakage?
    • How did you handle delayed or censored labels (for example a booking that happens weeks after a search)?
  2. How did you build the label, and how noisy was it? [Likely] [1]
    • Did you consider regression versus a bucketed classification framing? [Likely] [1]
  3. Which features mattered most, and how do you know? [Likely]
    • Did any "obviously useful" feature turn out to hurt?
  4. How did you keep training and serving features consistent? [Likely]
    • Did you ever ship with training-serving skew? How was it caught?
  5. How did you handle cold start for new users or new items? [Likely] [1]
  6. Did you use spatial or geographic signals? How did you encode them? [Likely] [1]
  7. Did your training data come from logs produced by an earlier model? How did you deal with feedback-loop and position bias? [Likely] [30]
  8. For a ranking or recommendation project, how did you build the training examples: impressions, negatives, sampling? [Likely] [34]
  9. Did the project use personal or sensitive data? What privacy, retention or fairness constraints shaped the features? [Likely]

E. Modeling decisions and trade-offs

  1. Walk me through the architecture and the key design points. [Reported] [6][23]
    • Why this design over the obvious alternative?
  2. What were the main trade-offs in your ML project? [Reported] [1][9][37]
    • What did you give up, and how did you decide it was acceptable?
  3. What alternatives did you consider and reject? [Reported] [2][3]
    • What evidence made you reject each one?
  4. Why this model family (for example GBDT versus a neural network)? [Likely] [1][30]
    • When did you decide to move to something more complex, and what convinced you?
    • Why this feature set and this serving path rather than the alternatives? [Likely] [1]
  5. What was the single modeling change that gave the biggest gain? [Likely]
    • What did you try that did not work? [Likely] [30]
  6. How did you tune the model, and how did you avoid overfitting to your validation set? [Likely]
  7. How did you trade model quality against latency, cost or interpretability? [Likely]
  8. Did downstream consumers depend on calibrated scores? How did you check calibration, and did it hold after launch? [Likely]
  9. If the project used embeddings or an LLM, how did you evaluate output quality, and how did you control inference cost? [Likely] [34]
  10. If the interviewer cannot follow your domain, how would you simplify the core idea in two minutes? [Reported] [8][14]

F. Architecture, serving and production

  1. Walk me through the implementation details end to end, from data pipeline to serving. [Reported] [6]
  2. What were the latency and throughput requirements at serving time, and how did you meet them? [Likely]
  3. Was inference batch, online or both? Why? [Likely]
  4. How did you deploy new model versions safely (shadow, canary, rollback)? [Likely]
  5. How did you make sure of system quality after deployment? [Reported] [17]
    • What monitoring and alerting did you set up for data drift and model decay?
  6. What broke in production, and how did you find out? [Likely]
  7. Which part of the system would break first at 10x scale? [Likely]
  8. Did you build or reuse a platform (feature store, training pipeline, experiment framework)? Why? [Likely]
  9. Walk me through an incident on this project. What was the root cause, and what was the fix? [Reported] [2]
  10. How often was the model retrained, and how did you choose that cadence? How were data and model versions tracked so that a run could be reproduced? [Likely]
  11. What did the project cost in compute and infrastructure, and how did you justify that cost? [Likely]

G. Offline-to-online evaluation and experimentation (A/B testing focus)

  1. How did you evaluate the model offline, and which metric did you trust? [Likely] [1]
    • How well did the offline metric predict the online result?
  2. How did an offline metric gain become an online metric gain? [Likely] [1]
  3. How did you know it worked? Was there an A/B test? [Likely] [31]
    • What was the primary metric, and what were the guardrails? [Likely] [34]
    • What was the randomization unit, and why?
    • How long did it run, and how did you choose the sample size?
  4. What were the confidence intervals on the main result? Were any segments surprising? [Likely]
  5. Did offline gains ever fail to show up online? How did you debug it? [Likely] [30]
  6. Did your experiment face interference effects (for example a two-sided marketplace or shared inventory)? How did you handle it? [Likely] [31][34]
    • Would cluster-level or market-level randomization have changed the answer? [Likely] [34]
  7. Did you consider faster methods such as interleaving for a ranking change? Why or why not? [Likely] [32]
  8. Did you use variance reduction such as CUPED? What did it buy you? [Likely]
  9. How did you handle novelty effects, seasonality or ramp-up effects when you read the result? [Likely]
  10. Did you check for sample ratio mismatch or logging bugs before you trusted the result? [Likely]
  11. Did you keep a long-term holdout? What did it show compared with the initial test? [Likely]
  12. Tell me about an experiment result that surprised you. What did you do next? [Likely]
  13. Who made the launch decision, and what would have made you not launch? [Likely]
  14. How did you choose the minimum detectable effect, and did you check beforehand that the metric was sensitive enough to detect it? [Likely]
  15. How did you handle many metrics and segments at once, and how did you avoid peeking or stopping the test early? [Likely]
  16. The true outcome (for example a completed stay, a review or long-term value) arrives weeks later. Which proxy or surrogate metric did you use in the test, and how did you validate it? [Likely] [6][34]
  17. If a clean A/B test was not possible (for example for pricing or a policy change), which quasi-experimental method did you use, and what assumptions did it rest on? [Likely] [34]
  18. The primary metric was flat but a secondary metric improved. What did you recommend, and why? [Likely]

H. Impact and metrics

  1. What was the measured impact, in business terms? [Reported] [2][3]
    • How did you attribute the impact to your work and not to other launches?
  2. How did you ensure the users actually benefited after deployment? [Reported] [17]
  3. What metrics did you use to define success, and who agreed to them? [Reported] [2]
    • How did you align Product and Data Science on the success metric? [Likely]
  4. Were there negative side effects on another metric or another group (for example hosts versus guests)? [Likely]
  5. If the impact was smaller than expected, why? [Likely]
  6. Did you measure segment-level harm, for example on new hosts, small markets or new users? What did you find? [Likely]

I. Challenges, failures and debugging

  1. What was the most challenging part of the project? [Reported] [16][15]
    • Was the challenge technical, organizational or about reliability?
  2. What technical challenges did you hit, and how did you solve them? [Reported] [6][14]
    • Where exactly is the technical challenge in this project? [Reported] [10]
  3. Tell me about a project or plan that missed its ETA, or whose scope had to shrink. [Reported] [9]
    • How did you identify the root cause? [Reported] [9]
    • Did you have a fallback plan to unblock the team? [Reported] [9]
  4. Tell me about a model that worked offline but failed in production. [Likely]
  5. Tell me about a bug or data problem that invalidated results. How was it found? [Likely]
  6. What was the hardest debugging problem on the project? [Likely]
  7. Tell me about a failure on this project and what you did next. [Reported] [3][4]
    • Tell me about a real mistake you made, not the polished version of the story. [Reported] [3]

J. Planning, milestones, collaboration and conflict

  1. How did you plan the project? [Reported] [15]
    • How did you set and deliver milestones? [Reported] [17][11][1]
  2. Describe the project's milestones, a conflict over scope, and how it was resolved. [Reported] [1]
  3. Describe a conflict during the project, and which of the rejected alternatives you would choose now. [Reported] [2]
  4. How did you scope the project to fit a deadline? [Reported] [2]
    • When requirements changed mid-project, how did you re-plan and rescope? [Reported] [2][3]
  5. How was the engineering effort structured? [Reported] [11][29]
  6. How did you persuade stakeholders to back the project? [Reported] [2]
    • Who opposed it, and what changed their mind?
  7. Did you have a conflict on the project? How did you resolve it? [Reported] [15][16][9]
    • What would the other person say about how it was resolved?
  8. How did you work with your teammates? [Reported] [16]
  9. Tell me about collaborating with another team where their delay affected you. [Reported] [9]
  10. How did you keep teams in different time zones or cultures aligned from design through implementation? What problems came up? (asked in the core values round) [Reported] [9]
  11. How did you handle a tight deadline or a complex project you owned? [Reported] [4]
  12. How did you work with data scientists on metric definitions and experiment reading? [Likely]
  13. How did you influence the technical direction without formal authority? [Likely]

K. Retrospective, learning and next steps

  1. If you redid the project, what would you change? [Reported] [15][17][3]
    • What would you change if you had to redesign it from scratch? [Reported] [17]
  2. What did you learn from the project? [Reported] [16]
  3. What would the next iteration of the system look like? [Likely]
  4. If you had half the time, what would you cut? [Likely]
  5. What technical debt did you leave behind, and why was it acceptable? [Likely]
  6. How did you decide the project was finished, and what did you stop doing? [Likely]
  7. What was the biggest obstacle in moving from writing code to owning a whole project? (asked in the core values round) [Reported] [9]

L. Leadership and senior/staff calibration

  1. Walk me through your experience, a past project and your leadership on it. [Reported] [21][36]
  2. How did you grow or mentor others on this project? [Likely]
  3. What is your current scope at work, and what scope are you looking for? [Reported] [10]
    • Are you looking to be a tech lead, or a strong IC? [Reported] [10]
  4. How did this project change how your team or organization works? [Likely]
  5. Which decision on this project would you defend most strongly to a skeptical staff engineer? [Likely]
  6. What was the strongest pushback in design review, and did it change the design? [Likely]
  7. How did you decide what not to build? [Likely]

M. Mapping your project to Airbnb

  1. How does your system compare with how Airbnb solved a similar problem, and why did you choose your approach? [Reported] [24]
    • Have you read Airbnb's engineering blog posts on search ranking, pricing or experimentation? How does your work differ? [Likely] [24][30][31]
  2. How would your project transfer to a two-sided marketplace like Airbnb's (guests and hosts)? [Likely]
  3. What in your background maps directly onto this team's problems? [Reported] [20][2]
    • You have also worked in an unrelated area (for example hardware). How is that relevant here? [Reported] [20]
  4. If you joined, what would you build in your first six months, based on this experience? [Likely]
  5. Tell me about a project connected to your personal or professional values. How did it embody or challenge them? [Reported] [19][33]

N. Pressure probes reported in the room

These are the push-back styles candidates describe. Practise staying concise and concrete under each one.

  1. "I still don't understand. Can you explain the task again, more simply?" [Reported] [14]
  2. "Why did you do that at all?" (repeated challenges to the rationale for a phase of the project) [Reported] [10]
  3. "Can you be more specific? What were the actual technical details?" (asked because answers sounded generic) [Reported] [18]
  4. Interruptions and nitpicking mid-answer: how do you regain the thread? [Reported] [27]
  5. "Let's switch: how would you design an ML system for X?" (the experience round turns into ML design) [Reported] [7]
  6. "Give me the specific API and database design of your system." (a detail-level probe instead of scaling talk; reported in an Airbnb system design round, not the deep dive) [Likely] [28]
